A Graphical Coaction for FRW Integrals from Partial/Relative Twisted (Co)homology Preprint: QMUL-PH-26-22
Andrew J. McLeod, Andrzej Pokraka, and Lecheng Ren

Abstract
We construct a graphical coaction for Friedmann-Robertson-Walker (FRW) integrals at all loop orders in conformally-coupled scalar theories with non-conformal polynomial interactions. Our construction makes use of intersection theory in the context of (partial/relative) twisted (co)homology, which we use to decompose FRW integrals (and their discontinuities and derivatives) into building blocks that can be represented as decorations of the original Feynman diagram. This facilitates a purely graphical description of the coaction, up to rational prefactors that can be read off from the graph. Our construction provides a comprehensive combinatorial framework for dissecting the analytic properties of cosmological observables; in particular, we demonstrate that the combinatorics of the differential equations that govern FRW integrals—their so-called kinematic flow—is a natural consequence of our coaction. We have also developed a user-friendly web application that computes the graphical coaction of any graph: ​. Whenever possible, the web application also computes the differentials and discontinuities. A Mathematica notebook with the same functionality is also hosted at the following repository: .



1
Introduction

In recent years, cosmology has emerged as a fertile ground for the application of ideas from algebraic geometry, combinatorics, and number theory. These mathematical perspectives have yielded new insight into the structure of cosmological observables, and—increasingly—the ways in which this structure is dictated by basic physical principles.
The result has been a rich interplay of ideas that continue to reveal deep mathematical principles at the heart of quantum field theory.

Many of these developments in cosmology have drawn inspiration from
advances in our understanding of scattering amplitudes, whose mathematical structure has been under intense investigation for over half a century
53
, from the classes of special functions amplitudes evaluate to
23
;
17
;
20
, to the ways in which their analytic structure is constrained by physical principles
73
;
84
;
85
;
79
;
66
;
34
and the discovery of unexpected number-theoretic symmetries
12
;
82
;
32
;
78
;
83
;
34
;
65
;
48
;
46
. One important facet of this research has focused on amplitudes as
motivic periods

60
;
25
;
31
, namely as (sums of) integrals whose analytic and algebraic properties can be investigated with the use of geometric tools from homology and cohomology.
In particular,
the
motivic coaction
is used to decompose an integral into simpler building blocks that faithfully capture the original integral’s analytic properties.
By iteratively applying the coaction, one can often decompose Feynman integrals into tensor products of one-fold integrals that can be evaluated in closed form (into functions such as logarithms). The upshot is that identities between the (highly nontrivial) special functions that arise in scattering amplitudes can be understood in terms of relations between these simpler functions. Using this technology, the analytic structure of amplitudes involving even billions of terms can be made comprehensible (see for instance
36
;
47
;
49
;
14
;
69
).

While the motivic coaction first appeared in the scattering amplitudes literature in connection with multiple polylogarithms (MPLs)
39
;
59
;
63
;
81
;
18
;
77
;
44
;
45
;
61
;
62
;
30
;
29
;
54
;
55
, coactions have been constructed/conjectured for more general classes of functions. These include elliptic polylogarithms
24
;
86
;
28
, hypergeometric functions
26
;
2
, and iterated integrals over holomorphic Eisenstein series
72
. Intriguingly, a
diagrammatic
coaction has also been defined for one-loop Feynman integrals
5
;
7
, which decomposes the integral associated with a given Feynman diagram into a basis of integrals associated with contracted and cut versions of the same graph—thereby translating the nontrivial algebraic and analytic properties of these integrals into simple graph-theoretic relations. Effort has gone into extending this diagrammatic coaction to two-loop Feynman integrals
3
, although a general formulation that works beyond one loop is not yet known.

In this paper, we define a similar graphical coaction on the integrals in cosmology that contribute to the Friedmann-Robertson-Walker (FRW) wavefunction of the universe, for any number of external edges and to all loop orders in conformally-coupled scalar theories with non-conformal polynomial interactions. In particular, we demonstrate that the mathematical objects that appear in the coaction of these integrals are in one-to-one correspondence with decorated graphs
{\mathfrak{g}}
involving pinched, broken, and directed edges (

,

,
and

), such that the directed edges do not form an oriented cycle. While the pinched and broken edges play a similar role to contracted and cut edges in the graphical coaction on Feynman integrals, the additional appearance of directed edges reflects the fundamental role that time ordering plays in cosmological perturbation theory.
This time ordering restricts which singularities actually occur in the FRW wavefunction (out of the ones that generically arise, for general integration contours).

To construct the graphical coaction, we adopt a geometric perspective in which FRW wavefunction coefficients—the building blocks of the FRW wavefunction—are expressed as sums of
twisted periods
.
Twisted periods are common in quantum field theory computations, and arise when factors in the integrand appear raised to general complex powers.
In this context, the FRW integrals that we encounter are of the form

\displaystyle F(\bm{\beta};\underline{\bm{l}}\bm{c}):=\int_{\Delta_{m}}\frac{z_{1}^{\beta_{1}}\cdots z_{m}^{\beta_{m}}(1-z_{1}-z_{2}-\cdots-z_{m})^{\beta_{m+1}}}{(\bm{l}_{1}\cdot\bm{z}-c_{1})(\bm{l}_{2}\cdot\bm{z}-c_{2})\cdots(\bm{l}_{m}\cdot\bm{z}-c_{m})}\mathrm{d}^{m}\bm{z}

(1.1)

where
\Delta_{m}
is the standard
m
-simplex, each
\bm{l}_{i}
is an
m
-component vector with entries drawn from
\{0,\pm 1\}
, and the powers
\beta_{i}
are complex numbers.
The argument
\underline{\bm{l}}\bm{c}:=\bm{l}_{1}\cdots\bm{l}_{n}\bm{c}
is an
(n{+}1)\times m
matrix whose columns are constituted by the vectors
\bm{l}
or
\bm{c}
.

Conveniently, there exists a natural (albeit conjectural) coaction on twisted periods that reduces the study of their analytic structure to knowledge of the corresponding twisted homology and cohomology groups, their Poincaré duals, and the associated set of positive geometries. Similar to the coactions that have been developed for Feynman integrals, this coaction for FRW integrals breaks each wavefunction coefficient into a sum over simpler building blocks.
In particular, when applied to the FRW integral associated with a graph
{\mathfrak{g}}
, the coaction can be phrased graphically as

\displaystyle\Delta({\mathfrak{g}})=\sum_{\text{compatible }{\mathfrak{h}}}C_{{\mathfrak{h}}{\mathfrak{h}}}^{-1}\;{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes\text{Disc}_{{\mathfrak{h}}}[{\mathfrak{g}}]\,,

(1.2)

where the
C_{{\mathfrak{h}}{\mathfrak{h}}}
are self-intersection numbers that evaluate to rational functions of the parameters of one’s theory (which can be pulled out of the tensor product), and the decorated graphs
{\mathfrak{h}}
represent different (often simpler) FRW integrals.
Importantly, the FRW integrals in the first component of this tensor product (multiplied by rational prefactors) constitute the derivatives of the original integral, while the integrals in the second component constitute the discontinuities of the original integral (modulo factors of
i\pi
).
When iterated, the coaction produces tensor products of arbitrary length (with each component other than the first interpreted modulo
i\pi
).

Before introducing the mathematical details that underlie this construction, let us illustrate it for the two-site chain graph.
First, we decompose the wavefunction coefficient associated with this graph into our basis of decorated graphs (each of which represents an FRW integral). In this case, we get

\displaystyle\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chain_ext}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chain_gr}+\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chain_gl}-\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chain_gb}\,.

(1.3)

Applying the coaction
\Delta
to these decorated graphs yields

\displaystyle\Delta\left(\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chain_ext}\right)

\displaystyle=\Delta\left(\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chain_gr}\right)+\Delta\left(\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chain_gl}\right)-\Delta\left(\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chain_gb}\right)

(1.4)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}\cdot\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chain_gs}\otimes\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chain_gs_gr}+1\cdot\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chain_gr}\otimes\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chainCr}

\displaystyle+\frac{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}\cdot\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chain_gs}\otimes\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chain_gs_gl}+1\cdot\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chain_gl}\otimes\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chainCl}

\displaystyle-1\cdot\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chain_gb}\otimes\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chainCb}\,,

where we have separated the parts of the expression that are generated by different FRW integrals onto different lines.
First, let us discuss the first component of these tensor products.
The graphs
{\mathfrak{h}}
that are compatible with the original graph
{\mathfrak{g}}
in (
1.2
) are those graphs that can be generated by replacing a subset of the oriented edges (
) with pinched edges (
), where we discard any graphs that have an oriented cycle after all pinched edges are contracted.
In the decomposition in (
1.3
), the first two decorated graphs are compatible with a pair of graphs (one either replaces the single oriented edge or not), while the third decorated graph is only compatible with itself (since it contains no directed edges that can be replaced).
To each of these compatible graphs
{\mathfrak{h}}
, we associate a unique cut graph that appears in the second component of the tensor product. These cuts are denoted by the
gray
tubes, which identify the maximal set of denominator factors that are compatible with the decorated graph in the first entry, and indicate that these denominators have all been placed on shell. We will provide a complete description of how to construct these sets of tubings below; for now, we just note that

•

no tubes cross pinched lines,

•

no tubes encircle broken lines,

•

tubes only cross directed edges where the directed edge points away from the tube.

Finally, the rational prefactors involving
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
arise from the self-intersection numbers
C_{{\mathfrak{h}}{\mathfrak{h}}}
, and only depend on the details of the underlying QFT and FRW spacetime.
We stress that no knowledge of intersection theory is needed to use the coaction, since we provide explicit formulas for all intersection numbers
C_{{\mathfrak{h}}{\mathfrak{h}}}
.

We again emphasize that each of the graphs in (
1.4
) represents an FRW integral, which evaluates to a generalized hypergeometric function of the type shown in (
1.1
). However, the FRW coaction (
1.2
) is also compatible with the more familiar coaction on MPLs, in the sense that it commutes with expanding FRW integrals in limits that evaluate (order by order) to MPLs. That is, one gets the same result by applying the coaction (
1.2
) and then expanding all tensor factors in terms of MPLs, or by first expanding the original FRW integral in terms of MPLs and then computing the MPL coaction of each term.

More formally, to construct the coaction in (
1.2
), we associate to each decorated graph
{\mathfrak{g}}
four (co)homological objects: a pair of cycles
\gamma_{\mathfrak{g}}
and
\check{\gamma}_{\mathfrak{g}}
, and a pair of cocycles
\varphi_{\mathfrak{g}}
and
\check{\varphi}_{\mathfrak{g}}
. Roughly speaking:

\gamma_{\mathfrak{g}}
:

Elements of the FRW homology
: integration contours for FRW integrals and their cuts (which encode the discontinuities of these integrals).

\varphi_{\mathfrak{g}}
:

Elements of the FRW cohomology
: the rational differential forms of FRW integrals, which can have poles where propagators go on-shell, or at the branch loci of the twist.

\check{\gamma}_{\mathfrak{g}}
:

Elements of the dual homology
:
these objects do not appear directly as integration contours, but are (Poincaré) dual to the FRW integrands
\varphi_{\mathfrak{g}}
.

\check{\varphi}_{\mathfrak{g}}
:

Elements of the dual cohomology
: differential forms that are (Poincaré) dual to the
\gamma_{\mathfrak{g}}
. These objects are used to compute the coefficients
C_{{\mathfrak{h}}{\mathfrak{h}}}
in (
1.2
).

As we will show, the graph
{\mathfrak{g}}
encodes all the information needed to build
\gamma_{\mathfrak{g}}
,
\varphi_{\mathfrak{g}}
,
\check{\gamma}_{\mathfrak{g}}
, and
\check{\varphi}_{\mathfrak{g}}
, as well as the set of compatible graphs
{\mathfrak{h}}
that are needed to compute the coaction (
1.2
).
The pairings between these four objects (and the canonical-form and intersection maps that relate them) are summarized in figure
3
of section
3.2
.

The remainder of this paper is structured as follows.
Section
2
reviews the computation of FRW wavefunction coefficients as twisted periods. This includes a discussion of the peculiarities of the geometry underlying FRW integrals, and the development of combinatorial tools for organizing this geometry.
In section
3
, we review the properties and utility of the coaction as well as how it is constructed from twisted (co)homology and intersection theory.
Sections
2
and
3
provide all the needed machinery for understanding the examples in section
5
without delving into the details of twisted (co)homology or intersection theory.
Readers interested in understanding the full geometric construction of the FRW (co)homology and their duals in the partial/relative twisted setting can find this material in section
4
.
There, the details are spelled out with the two-site chain graph as a pedagogical example.

2
FRW integrals

In this work, we study conformally-coupled scalar field theories in FRW cosmologies with non-conformal polynomial interactions. The action in
(d+1)
spacetime dimensions is

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}=\int\mathrm{d}^{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\eta\sqrt{-g}\left[-\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial_{\nu}\phi-\frac{d-1}{8d}R\phi^{2}-\sum_{p\geq 3}\frac{\lambda_{p}}{p!}\phi^{p}\right]~,

(2.1)

where the FRW metric is
\mathrm{d}s^{2}=a^{2}(\eta)\left[-\mathrm{d}\eta^{2}+\mathrm{d}x_{i}\mathrm{d}x^{i}\right]
in comoving coordinates with conformal time
\eta\in(-\infty,0]
, and
i
should be understood as running over all spatial coordinates
i=1,\dots,d
. The scale factor is chosen to be a power-law characterized by the cosmological parameter
\varepsilon
,

a(\eta)=(\eta/\eta_{0})^{-(1+\varepsilon)}\,.

(2.2)

This power-law specializes to several well-studied cosmological scenarios, such as de Sitter (
\varepsilon=0
), flat (
\varepsilon=-1
), radiation-dominated (
\varepsilon=-2
), and matter-dominated (
\varepsilon=-3
) universes
43
;
10
; moreover, for
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
this model is expected to capture the essential dynamics of near de Sitter inflationary cosmology.

The main quantities of interest in these theories are equal-time correlation functions—these functions encode the quantum fluctuations present at the end of inflation, which ultimately seed the temperature and density variations observed today.
Conveniently, for theories with the action in (
2.1
), the perturbative contributions to these correlation functions take a universal form. Namely, the integral that we associate with each Feynman diagram in the flat-space theory can be upgraded to any power-law FRW spacetime with
\varepsilon\notin\mathbb{Q}
through the inclusion of a twist factor
u_{\mathcal{G}}
:

\displaystyle\psi_{\mathcal{G}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}u_{\mathcal{G}}\ \varphi_{\mathcal{G}}\,,

(2.3)

where

\displaystyle\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}=\hat{\Omega}_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})\;\mathrm{d}^{n}\mathbf{x}

(2.4)

and
\hat{\Omega}_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})

is the flat-space wavefunction coefficient of the flat-space integrand associated with the (truncated) Feynman graph
\mathcal{G}
. It depends on the energies
X_{v}
and
Y_{e}
that enter each vertex
v
and flow through each edge
e
, respectively. (We denote the collection of each set of variables by
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{Y}
.) These energies all take positive values in the physical region. Denoting the set of vertices in
\mathcal{G}
by
\mathcal{V}
, the twist factor

\displaystyle u_{\mathcal{G}}=\prod_{v\in\mathcal{V}}x_{v}^{\alpha_{v}}

(2.5)

is a multi-valued function whose exponents
\alpha_{v}
are determined by the underlying power-law cosmology (as specified by
\varepsilon
), the number of spatial dimensions
d
, and the valency
p_{v}
of each vertex
v
:

\displaystyle\alpha_{v}=d+\varepsilon(d+1)+\frac{1}{2}p_{v}(1+\varepsilon)(1-d)\,.

(2.6)

Our assumption that
\varepsilon\notin\mathbb{Q}
guarantees that
\alpha_{v}\notin\mathbb{Z}
, which places us in the context of twisted cohomology.

In more detail, the flat-space
integrand
\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}

is a rational function of the variables
x_{v}
,
X_{v}
, and
Y_{e}
that inherits a
simple combinatorial definition from the flat space wavefunction
\hat{\Omega}_{\mathcal{G}}
, namely

\displaystyle\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}=\mathrm{d}^{|\mathcal{V}|}\mathbf{x}\;\sum_{T_{\mathcal{G}}}\prod_{\tau\in T_{\mathcal{G}}}\frac{1}{B_{\tau}}\,,

(2.7)

where
T_{\mathcal{G}}
denotes the set of complete tubings of
\mathcal{G}
. The denominator factors
B_{\tau}
are the basic atoms of the on-shell variety and appear throughout the paper; we collect their definition in box
2
.

Box 2.1: Tubes and tube polynomials

A
tube

\tau
on
\mathcal{G}
is a simple closed curve encircling a subset of
\mathcal{G}
, specified by a pair of sets

\tau=\{\mathcal{V}_{\tau},\,\mathcal{E}_{\tau}\}\,,

where
\mathcal{V}_{\tau}
is the set of vertices of
\mathcal{G}
encircled by
\tau
and
\mathcal{E}_{\tau}
is the set of edges of
\mathcal{G}
that
\tau
crosses. A
complete tubing
is a maximal set of non-intersecting tubes; the set of complete tubings of
\mathcal{G}
is denoted
T_{\mathcal{G}}
. To each tube
\tau
we associate the
tube polynomial

\displaystyle B_{\tau}\;=\;\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}}\left(x_{v}+X_{v}\right)\;+\;\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\tau}}Y_{e}\,.

(2.8)

These polynomials are the denominators in (
2.7
); their zero loci
\{B_{\tau}=0\}
make up the on-shell variety
\mathcal{B}
and govern the cut structure used throughout the paper.

As an example, consider the three-site chain graph

\displaystyle\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain.pdf}\,.

(2.9)

It has the following tubes and associated tube polynomials:

\tau

B_{\tau}

\tau

B_{\tau}

x_{1}+X_{1}+Y_{12}

x_{1}+x_{2}+X_{1}+X_{2}+Y_{23}

x_{2}+X_{2}+Y_{12}+Y_{23}

x_{2}+x_{3}+X_{2}+X_{3}+Y_{12}

x_{3}+X_{3}+Y_{23}

x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}+X_{1}+X_{2}+X_{3}

The full integrand for this diagram can be constructed in terms of these tube polynomials by summing over the two complete tubings

T_{\mathcal{G}}=\left\{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainCompleteTube1.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainCompleteTube2.pdf}\right\}\,.

(2.14)

For more details, see
11
.

More formally, we think of the integral (
2.3
) as a period, namely the pairing of a
partially twisted cycle

[\gamma_{\mathcal{G}}|

with a
partially twisted cocycle

|\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}\rangle
:

\displaystyle\psi_{\mathcal{G}}:=\int_{0}^{\infty}u_{\mathcal{G}}\;\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}=:[\gamma_{\mathcal{G}}|\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}\rangle\,.

(2.15)

The bracket types in
[\gamma_{\mathcal{G}}|\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}\rangle
are not decorative: square and angle brackets each encode whether the corresponding slot holds a homology or cohomology class. The three pairings used throughout the paper all follow this rule, and we collect them in box
2
for reference.

Box 2.2: Bra-ket conventions

Rule.
In any bracket pairing, a
square
bracket marks a (co)homology slot containing a
cycle
(homology class), while an
angle
bracket marks a slot containing a
cocycle
(cohomology class). The argument inside each bra or ket is one representative of its equivalence class.

The three pairings that appear in this paper, together with their short names, are:

Pairing

Notation

Short name

period (integration)

[\gamma_{\mathfrak{g}}|\varphi_{\mathfrak{h}}\rangle=\int_{\gamma_{\mathfrak{g}}}u_{\mathcal{G}}\,\varphi_{\mathfrak{h}}

P_{{\mathfrak{g}}{\mathfrak{h}}}

cohomology intersection

\langle\check{\varphi}_{\mathfrak{g}}|\varphi_{\mathfrak{h}}\rangle

C_{{\mathfrak{g}}{\mathfrak{h}}}

homology intersection

[\gamma_{\mathfrak{g}}|\check{\gamma}_{\mathfrak{h}}]

As we will see,
\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}
has poles outside of the vanishing loci of the twist
u_{\mathcal{G}}
, which is why we refer to it as
partially
twisted.
In this language, it becomes natural to extend our attention to the complete set of integrals that can be constructed from the geometry that defines
\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}
and
u_{\mathcal{G}}
. More specifically, given any Feynman graph
\mathcal{G}
we can construct a pair of varieties:

•

The
twisted
variety,
\mathcal{T}=V(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})
.
This is the locus where the twist
u_{\mathcal{G}}
vanishes.

•

The
on-shell
variety,
\mathcal{B}=V(B_{\tau_{1}}\cdots B_{\tau_{N}})
; the loci where the propagators
B_{\tau}
(the hyperplanes that appear in the denominator of
\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}
) vanish.

Given these varieties, we define a pair of topological spaces

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}:=M\setminus\mathcal{B}\,,\qquad M:=\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus\mathcal{T}\,,

(2.16)

in which the relevant hyperplanes have been excised.
We then consider the set of partially twisted cycles
[\gamma_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}}|
that do not intersect
\mathcal{B}
, and that end on
\mathcal{T}
.
These cycles can be paired with a dual set of cocycles
|\varphi_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}}\rangle

to define the larger set of periods

\displaystyle P_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{1},{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}}:=\int_{\gamma_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}}}u_{\mathcal{G}}\;\varphi_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}}=:[\gamma_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}}|\varphi_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}}\rangle\,.

(2.17)

As we will see later, each of the
|\varphi_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}}\rangle
can be chosen to be the canonical form of one of the bounded chambers in
\mathcal{M}
(meaning that, like
|\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}\rangle
, these forms can have poles on both
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{T}
).

It has been shown that all kinematic derivatives of the integral (
2.3
) can be expressed in terms of a strict subset of the family of periods that take the form (
2.17
)
57
;
10
;
15
;
70
.
That is, the number of periods that are actually relevant to physics is generally much smaller than the number of elements in the full (co)homology. The relevant cohomological subspace is spanned by the physical wavefunction coefficient
\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}
and its derivatives,

\displaystyle H^{n}_{\mathrm{phys}}:=\mathrm{Span}\{\varphi_{\mathcal{G}},\partial_{X_{\bullet}}^{\mathbb{N}}\partial_{Y_{\bullet}}^{\mathbb{N}}\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}\}\,,

(2.18)

while the homological subspace
H_{n}^{\mathrm{phys}}
is spanned by contours that take cuts which are compatible with the elements of
H^{n}_{\mathrm{phys}}
. We take advantage of this fact, by constructing a graphical coaction that only invokes elements of the (co)homology that appear in this physical sector.
1
1

1

More specifically, while the coaction can be straightforwardly extended to include any period of the form (
2.17
), the
graphical
coaction that we construct is coextensive with just the physical subspace.

In particular, we describe how to construct a basis for the physical subspace of the partially twisted cycles
[\gamma_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}}|
and cocycles
|\varphi_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}}\rangle
that can be put in one-to-one correspondence with certain decorations of the graph
\mathcal{G}
.

In the remainder of this section, we introduce the minimal background material needed to construct the graphical coaction.
First, in section
2.1
, we identify where degenerate configurations of the cosmological hyperplane arrangements
\mathcal{B}
occur, and how this affects the partially twisted (co)homology of
\mathcal{M}
.
We then describe, in section
2.2
, how this degeneracy can be resolved in the physical sector by considering certain decorations of the graph
\mathcal{G}
.
In particular, these decorated graphs are in one-to-one correspondence with the set of cuts (or residues) that are nonzero for the wavefunction coefficient. In section
2.3
, we describe how to construct a positive geometry for each of these cuts by restricting the twisted hyperplanes
\mathcal{T}
to the cut.
As we will see, each physical cut and its associated positive geometry corresponds to a basis element of the physical FRW homology.
Moreover, each physical cut also defines an element in the
dual
homology whose canonical form provides a basis element for the FRW cohomology.

2.1
Degenerate hyperplane arrangements

The tube polynomials
B_{\tau}
defined in (
2.8
) are not linearly independent; whenever a pair of tubes overlap, the corresponding
B_{\tau}
satisfy a linear relation.
In such cases, the corresponding hyperplane arrangement
\mathcal{B}
is said to be degenerate.
2
2

2

On the other hand, the hyperplanes defining
\mathcal{T}
are always in a generic arrangement; they meet transversely.

As a result, the naive sequential residues are redundant. Thus, before we can assign a basis of residue operators to a graph, we have to resolve these redundancies—this is the goal of the subsection.

Whenever a pair of tubes
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
intersect, there exists a relation

\displaystyle B_{\tau_{1}}+B_{\tau_{2}}=B_{\tau_{1}\cup\tau_{2}}+B_{\tau_{1}\cap\tau_{2}}\,.

(2.19)

Thus, such a relation arises whenever we have partially-overlapping tubes; for instance, for the following pair of partially-overlapping two-site tubes, we generate the relation:

\displaystyle\underset{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainCrossed.pdf}}{\underbrace{B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT12.pdf}}+B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT23.pdf}}}}=\underset{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainUncrossed.pdf}}{\underbrace{B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT2.pdf}}+B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT123.pdf}}}}\,.

(2.20)

Because of the relations generated by (
2.19
), the intersection of
|K|
hyperplanes
\smash{\{\mathcal{B}_{k}\}_{k\in K}}
does not necessarily define a codimension-
|K|
hypersurface.
For example, for the three-site chain, equation (
2.20
) implies that setting any three of the polynomials

\{B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT123.pdf}},B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT12.pdf}},B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT23.pdf}},B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT2.pdf}}\}

(2.21)

to zero automatically sets the fourth to zero as well.
Geometrically, this means that the four hyperplanes
defined by these polynomials intersect at a point in the (three-dimensional) space of integration variables
\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\}
.
This non-genericity generates identities between differential forms and sequential residues; for instance, one can check that the following identity holds at the level of
\mathrm{dlog}
-forms:

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT123.pdf}}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT12.pdf}}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT23.pdf}}

(2.22)

\displaystyle-\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT123.pdf}}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT12.pdf}}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT2.pdf}}

\displaystyle+\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT123.pdf}}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT23.pdf}}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT2.pdf}}

\displaystyle-\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT12.pdf}}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT23.pdf}}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT2.pdf}}\,.

Similarly, at the level of sequential residues, it is easy to see that

\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT123.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT12.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT2.pdf}}=\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT123.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT12.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT23.pdf}}\,,

(2.23)

since (
2.20
) implies that

B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT23.pdf}}|_{B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT123.pdf}}=B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT12.pdf}}=0}=B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT2.pdf}}|_{B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT123.pdf}}=B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT12.pdf}}=0}

and consequently that
\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT2.pdf}}
and
\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT23.pdf}}
are equivalent once we have computed
\smash{\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT123.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT23.pdf}}}
. Note that our convention for sequential residues is

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\cdots,\tau_{m}}:=\text{Res}_{\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{m},\dots,\tau_{1}}}\circ\cdots\circ\text{Res}_{\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}}\circ\text{Res}_{\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{1}}}

(2.24)

where each residue operator acts to the right, and we recall that
\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{m}}=V(B_{\tau_{1}})\cap\cdots\cap V(\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{m}})
is the intersection of the hyperplanes generated by
B_{\tau_{1}},\dots,B_{\tau_{m}}
.
Denoting residues by the corresponding hyperplane intersections—which are coordinate independent—removes any coordinate ambiguity.

To construct a coaction, we would like to resolve these degeneracies by identifying sets of non-redundant sequential residues. To do so, we make use of the following result:

Box 2.3: Spanning Residues

The set of all sequential residue operators is spanned by those that respect a chosen global ordering of the tubes.

This statement is obvious when the residue hyperplanes are generic (namely, when the corresponding tube polynomials are linearly independent).
In this case, sequential residue operators are fully anti-symmetric in the sense that
\text{Res}_{i,j,k,\dots}=\text{sign}(\sigma)\text{Res}_{\sigma(i,j,k,\dots)}
for any permutation
\sigma
.
Therefore, one can span the space of sequential residue operators using only those that respect a global ordering.
Conversely, in non-generic arrangements, some sequential residue operators satisfy linear relations and will not be totally antisymmetric. Specifically, given any minimal collection of linearly dependent tube polynomials
\{B_{\tau_{j}}\}_{j=1}^{J}
(meaning all subsets with cardinality
|J|-1
are linearly independent) equations (
2.22
) and (
2.23
) generalize to

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{J}(-1)^{|J|-j}\bigwedge_{k\in J\setminus\{j\}}\mathrm{dlog}B_{k}=0\,,

(2.25)

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{\tau_{1},\cdots,\tau_{|J|-2},\tau_{|J|-1}}=\text{Res}_{\tau_{1},\cdots,\tau_{|J|-2},\tau_{|J|}}\,,

(2.26)

where the order of tubes
\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{|J|}
is compatible with the chosen global tube order.
Since the existence of the additional relations (
2.26
) can only reduce the number of independent operators needed to span the full set, the statement in Box
2.1
must still hold.

When considering the wavefunction of the universe, some orderings are better than others. To illustrate this, consider the following random ordering of the tubes that appear in the three-site chain:

(\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT123.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT2.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT12.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT23.pdf})
.
From (
2.26
), we have that two of the non-crossed tubings with this ordering are equal, namely

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT123.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT2.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT12.pdf}}=\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT123.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT2.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT23.pdf}}\,.

(2.27)

Moreover, this pair of non-crossed residues annihilate the physical form, since

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT123.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT2.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT12.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain.pdf}}]

\displaystyle\propto\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT123.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT2.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT12.pdf}}\left[\frac{B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT12.pdf}}+B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT23.pdf}}}{B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT2.pdf}}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT12.pdf}}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT23.pdf}}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT123.pdf}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT12.pdf}}\left[\frac{B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT12.pdf}}+B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT23.pdf}}}{B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT12.pdf}}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT23.pdf}}}\right]_{B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT2.pdf}}=B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT123.pdf}}=0}=0\,,

(2.28)

where in the last line above we have used (
2.20
) to set the numerator to zero. At the same time, for one of the crossed tubings, we have that

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT2.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT12.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT23.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain.pdf}}]

\displaystyle=\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT23.pdf}}\left[\frac{1}{B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT123.pdf}}}\right]_{B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT2.pdf}}=B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT12.pdf}}=0}

(2.29)

\displaystyle=\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT23.pdf}}\left[\frac{1}{B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT23.pdf}}}\right]_{B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT2.pdf}}=B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT12.pdf}}=0}=\frac{1}{Y_{12}Y_{23}}\,.

(2.30)

We conclude that this choice of ordering spoils the distinguished role that non-crossed tubings play in the construction of
\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}
(recall that each term in the wavefunction
\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}
is associated with a maximal
non-crossing
tubing
11
).

It turns out that a preferred ordering, in which the residues associated with crossed tubings can be ignored, does exist.
3
3

3

Note that changing the order of the tubes can only alter (
2.22
) by an overall sign. Thus, the identity among forms (
2.25
) is less sensitive to ordering changes than the residue operators (
2.26
).

The key is to order tubes that encircle more vertices before tubes that encircle fewer vertices; we break ties by ordering tubes that encircle the largest number of edges first, and then lexicographically by the minimal non-shared vertex.
4
4

4

One could instead choose the opposite ordering, in which tubes that encircle fewer vertices come first.
However, we prefer to compute the residues associated to
larger tubes first.
With this ordering, computing residues of the wavefunction reduces it to an amplitude or several sub-amplitudes
11
.

Then, the sequential residue associated to each crossed tubing is either:

•

equivalent to some linear combination of non-crossed residue operators, or

•

annihilates the physical FRW-form
\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}
.

Adopting this convention automatically reduces the number of residue operators—and, as demonstrated below, the number of elements of the (co)homology—needed to construct the coaction of
\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}
.
We emphasize that this set of residue operators is designed to span the space relevant to the wavefunction coefficient (as outlined in (
2.18
)), rather than the complete space of objects that can be defined on
\mathcal{M}
.

Putting everything together, and adopting a lexicographical ordering to order tubes of the same size, our tube ordering is given as follows:

Box 2.4: Tube Ordering

We always use sequential residues where the tubes are ordered such that

(1)

tubes that encircle more vertices come before tubes that encircle fewer vertices;

(2)

tubes that encircle more edges come before tubes that encircle fewer edges;

(3)

tubes that encircle the same number of vertices and edges are ordered lexicographically via the minimal vertex not shared by both.

In this ordering, the space of sequential residue operators is spanned by the set of operators associated with non-crossing tubings.

To close this subsection, we highlight that we can diagonalize the space of degenerate residues with respect to the physical FRW-form by a rotation of the independent residue operators.
For instance, in the degenerate sector of the three-site chain, we define

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{-}:=\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/3Chain123_12_2.pdf}}-\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/3Chain123_23_2.pdf}}\,,\qquad\mathrm{and}\qquad\text{Res}_{+}:=\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/3Chain123_12_2.pdf}}+\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/3Chain123_23_2.pdf}}\,,

(2.31)

after which the space of residues is spanned by
\text{Res}_{\pm}
and
\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/3Chain12_23_2.pdf}}
.
Moreover, in the new basis, only
\text{Res}_{-}
does not annihilate
\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}
:

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{-}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain.pdf}}]=\frac{1}{4Y_{12}Y_{23}}\,,\qquad\text{Res}_{+}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain.pdf}}]=0\,,\qquad\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/3Chain12_23_2.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain.pdf}}]=0\,.

(2.32)

This is a general feature; one can always organize the space of residue operators in a degenerate sector so that all but one of these operators annihilates
\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}
.
In the next section, we will develop a combinatorial algorithm for constructing this distinguished operator. We can then safely ignore all other residue operators in this sector, as they are unphysical.

2.2
Cut degeneracy and decorated graphs

Using the ideas from the last section, it is already possible to enumerate an independent basis of sequential residue operators that take into account the linear relations generated by (
2.19
), for any FRW integral
\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}
.
However, we would like to automate this construction as much as possible.
For this purpose, we consider different ways of decorating the graph
\mathcal{G}
.
As we will show, a basis of residue operators that do not annihilate the FRW form can be chosen that are in one-to-one correspondence with the
acyclic minors
of
\mathcal{G}
.

The acyclic minors of a graph
\mathcal{G}
are constructed by assigning decorations to each of its edges:

Box 2.5: Edge decorations

Decoration

Bulk physics interpretation

oriented

Energy (or time) flows in the direction of the arrow.

pinched

The connected vertices merge into a single contact interaction.

broken

No particles are exchanged between the two vertices.

Since the resulting graph is (partially) oriented, we refer to these graphs as
decorated orientations
of
\mathcal{G}
. We denote the set of all such decorated orientations of
\mathcal{G}
by
\text{Dec}(\mathcal{G})
.
It is clear that
|\text{Dec}(\mathcal{G})|=4^{|\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{G}}|}
, where
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{G}}
is the set of edges of
\mathcal{G}
.
A decorated orientation
{\mathfrak{g}}\in\text{Dec}(\mathcal{G})
is
acyclic
if the oriented graph produced by deleting all broken edges and contracting all pinched edges does not contain any cycles.
We denote the set of all
acyclic minors
by
\text{aDec}(\mathcal{G})\subseteq\text{Dec}(\mathcal{G})
.
5
5

5

The pinching (contracting) and breaking (removing) edges of
\mathcal{G}
corresponds to minors of graph
\mathcal{G}
in the usual mathematical sense; the only difference is that we have given each minor an acyclic orientation.

For tree level graphs we simply have
\text{aDec}(\mathcal{G})=\text{Dec}(\mathcal{G})
.

It turns out that we can associate a set of “physical” cut tubings
{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}
to each acyclic decorated graph
{\mathfrak{g}}\in\text{aDec}(\mathcal{G})
, according to the following rules:

Box 2.6: From acyclic decorated graphs to “physical” cut-tubings

Each tubing
\mathsf{c}\in{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}
is a maximal collection of non-crossing tubes where

(1)

no
\tau\in\mathsf{c}
crosses a pinched edges of
{\mathfrak{g}}
,

(2)

no
\tau\in\mathsf{c}
encircles a broken edge of
{\mathfrak{g}}
,

(3)

any
\tau\in\mathsf{c}
crosses an edge
e
only if

(a)

e
is a broken edge of
{\mathfrak{g}}
,

(b)

or
e
is an oriented edge
with
v_{1}\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}
and
v_{2}\not\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau}
.

For many acyclic minors, there is a unique tubing that satisfies the conditions in Box
2.2
.
As an example, consider the following acyclic minors of the four-site chain and the corresponding set of cut-tubings

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} {\mathsf{C}}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/lbs.pdf}}=\left\{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/Clbs.pdf}\right\}\,,\qquad{\mathsf{C}}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/sbs.pdf}}=\left\{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/Csbs.pdf}\right\}\,.\end{aligned}

(2.33)

There are also many acyclic decorated graphs that have multiple tubings
\mathsf{c}
, each of which satisfy the rules of Box
2.2
.
Some examples for the four-site chain are

\displaystyle{\mathsf{C}}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/lrr.pdf}}

\displaystyle=\left\{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/Clrr_1.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/Clrr_2.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/Clrr_3.pdf}\right\}\,,

(2.34)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{C}}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/lrs.pdf}}

\displaystyle=\left\{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/Clrs_1.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/Clrs_2.pdf}\right\}\,,

(2.35)

There are 60 more acyclic decorated orientations for the four-site chain, each of which gives rise to its own set of cut tubings.

Next, we associate a distinguished sequential residue operator to each
{\mathfrak{g}}\in\text{aDec}(\mathcal{G})
.
For decorations that give rise to a unique cut-tubing, this correspondence is clear—we just construct a sequential residue operator out of the set of tubes that appear in this cut-tubing, where the order of residues is dictated by our chosen tube ordering in Box
2.1
.
Note that, in general, these sequential residue operators will have different lengths; for instance, consider the following pair of examples:

\displaystyle\text{Res}{\mathsf{C}}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/sbs.pdf}}

\displaystyle=\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/Csbs.pdf}}:=\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/4ChainB12.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/4ChainB34.pdf}}\,,

(2.36)

\displaystyle\text{Res}{\mathsf{C}}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/lbs.pdf}}

\displaystyle=\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/Clbs.pdf}}:=\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/4ChainB12.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/4ChainB34.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/4ChainB2.pdf}}\,.

(2.37)

Only two tubes are associated with

,
while three tubes are associated with

.

The distinguished sequential residue operator that we associate with each acyclic decorated graph that has multiple cut-tubings is slightly more complicated.
To construct these residue operators, we first note that all the cut-tubings associated to an acyclic decorated graph specify the same variety.
Namely, for any pair
\mathsf{c},\mathsf{c}^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{C}}
that are given by
\mathsf{c}=\{\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{k}\}
and
\mathsf{c}^{\prime}=\{\tau_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\tau_{k}^{\prime}\}
, we have that

\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{c}}:=V(B_{\tau_{1}})\cap\cdots\cap V(B_{\tau_{k}})=V(B_{\tau_{1}^{\prime}})\cap\cdots\cap V(B_{\tau_{k}^{\prime}})=:\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{c}^{\prime}}\,.

(2.38)

In other words, each
\mathsf{c}\in{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}
just corresponds to a different way of writing the same linear system of polynomial equations, using identities of the form (
2.19
).
As a result, we associate a unique
cut space
to each decorated graph
{\mathfrak{g}}\in\text{aDec}(\mathcal{G})
:

\displaystyle M_{\mathfrak{g}}:=M\cap\mathcal{B}_{{\mathfrak{g}}}=(\mathbb{C}^{|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{G}}|}\setminus\mathcal{T})\cap\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{g}}\,,

(2.39)

where we have adopted the notation
\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{g}}:=\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{c}}\;\forall\;\mathsf{c}\in{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}
.
This codimension-
|\mathsf{c}|
space is the complement of
\mathcal{T}
on the intersection of the hyperplanes
\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{g}}
.
Since the polynomials
B_{\tau_{i}}
that appear in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{g}}
are all linear and (by construction) correspond to non-crossing tubes, they identify a single bounded chamber in
M_{\mathfrak{g}}
.
6
6

6

In fact, the bounded chamber on
M_{\mathfrak{g}}=M\cap\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{c}\in{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}}
is a Cartesian product of simplices.
This is not the case for cut spaces
M\cap\mathcal{B}_{\tau}
that are defined by collections of tubings
\tau
that cannot be associated to an acyclic decorated graph via the rules outlined in Box
2.2
.

(We will have more to say about this bounded chamber in section
2.3
.)
It follows that the twisted (co)homology on
M_{\mathfrak{g}}
is one-dimensional. This matches what was indicated at the end of section
2.1
, namely that the space of inequivalent sequential residue operators that localize us to
\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{g}}
can always be rotated so that all but one of the operators in this sector annihilate
\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}
. Therefore, without loss of generality, we associate the unique operator that acts nontrivially on
\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}
with the acyclic minor
\{\text{Res}_{\mathsf{c}}\}_{\mathsf{c}\in{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}}
.
It can be shown that this distinguished residue operator always takes the form

\displaystyle\text{Res}\,{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{c}\in{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\sgn_{\mathsf{c}}\text{Res}_{\mathsf{c}}\,,

(2.40)

where
\sgn_{\mathsf{c}}
is determined by comparing the signature of some ordered sets.
(While the explicit formula for
\sgn_{\mathsf{c}}
is not complicated, it depends on notation that distracts from the current exposition; it is given later in (
2.78
).)
Some simple examples include

\displaystyle\text{Res}{\mathsf{C}}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/lrr.pdf}}

\displaystyle=-\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/Clrr_1.pdf}}+\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/Clrr_2.pdf}}-\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/Clrr_3.pdf}}\,,

(2.41)

\displaystyle\text{Res}{\mathsf{C}}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/lrs.pdf}}

\displaystyle=\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/Clrs_1.pdf}}-\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/Clrs_2.pdf}}\,.

(2.42)

The operator
\text{Res}_{-}
, as defined in (
2.31
) for the three-site chain, constitutes another example.

We end this section by describing the inverse of the map in box
2.2
, by means of which we can construct the acyclic decorated graph
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathsf{c}}
associated with any physical tubing
\mathsf{c}
.
Namely, for each edge

in the graph
\mathcal{G}
, we assign the following decoration:

(1)

oriented as
,
if all the tubes in
\mathsf{c}
crossing it are encircling
v_{1}
;

(2)

oriented as
,
if all the tubes in
\mathsf{c}
crossing it are encircling
v_{2}
;

(3)

broken
,
if
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are both encircled by the tubes crossing the edge;

(4)

pinched
,
if there is no tube in
\mathsf{c}
crossing it.

It can be checked that, with these assignments, all the elements in
{\mathsf{C}}_{{\mathfrak{g}}}
map back to
{\mathfrak{g}}
itself.

2.3
The positive geometry of physical cuts

Having associated a set of tubings with each acyclic decorated graph
{\mathfrak{g}}
, we now also introduce a notion of graph regions:

Box 2.7: Regions of an acyclic decorated graph

To every
acyclic decorated graph
{\mathfrak{g}}
, we assign a set of graph regions
\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{g}}:=\{\mathsf{r}_{\bullet}\}
that partition the vertices in
{\mathfrak{g}}
so that each vertex is assigned to a unique region.
Each region
\mathsf{r}
is a connected subgraph of
{\mathfrak{g}}
that either consists of only pinched edges (
), or a single vertex that is not connected to a pinched edge.

The regions
\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{g}}
of
{\mathfrak{g}}
do not match the sets of vertices that are enclosed by the tubes in
{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}
. To illustrate this, we consider the following example:

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{g}}=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/g_dblbox_labeled.pdf}\,.

(2.43)

This acyclic graph has three regions, which we assign different colors for convenience:

\displaystyle\mathsf{r}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/regionExample_r123.pdf}\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{r}_{2}

\displaystyle:=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/regionExample_r4.pdf}\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{r}_{3}

\displaystyle:=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/regionExample_r56.pdf}\,,

(2.44)

Overlaying these regions on top of this graph’s (unique) cut tubing, we have:

\displaystyle B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/dblBoxB1234.pdf}}

\displaystyle=x_{1}{+}x_{2}{+}x_{3}{+}x_{4}{+}X_{1}{+}X_{2}{+}X_{3}{+}X_{4}{+}Y_{16}{+}Y_{36}{+}Y_{45}\,,

(2.45)

\displaystyle B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/dblBoxB4.pdf}}

\displaystyle=x_{4}{+}X_{4}{+}Y_{34}{+}Y_{45}\,,

\displaystyle B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/dblBoxB56.pdf}}

\displaystyle=x_{5}{+}x_{6}{+}X_{5}{+}X_{6}{+}Y_{16}{+}Y_{36}{+}Y_{45}\,.

Thus, while the regions do not correspond to the tubes appearing in the cut tubing directly, the set of vertices in each region are bounded by these tubes. While in some cases, the vertices in a region are bounded by a single outer tube (in this example,
\mathsf{r}_{2}
and
\mathsf{r}_{3}
), others are also bounded by inner tubes (
\mathsf{r}_{1}
).

If we rearrange the on-shell conditions that come from setting all three of the tube polynomials in (
2.45
) to zero, we see that

\displaystyle\partial\mathsf{r}_{1}=B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/dblBoxB1234.pdf}}-B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/dblBoxB4.pdf}}=0

\displaystyle\implies x_{1}{+}x_{2}{+}x_{3}{+}X_{1}{+}X_{2}{+}X_{3}{+}Y_{16}{+}Y_{36}{-}Y_{34}=0\,,

(2.46)

\displaystyle\partial\mathsf{r}_{2}=B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/dblBoxB4.pdf}}=0

\displaystyle\implies x_{4}{+}X_{4}{+}Y_{34}{+}Y_{45}=0\,,

\displaystyle\partial\mathsf{r}_{3}=B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/dblBoxB56.pdf}}=0

\displaystyle\implies x_{5}{+}x_{6}{+}X_{5}{+}X_{6}{+}Y_{16}{+}Y_{36}{+}Y_{45}=0\,.

Written in this form, it becomes clear that the on-shell conditions place a single linear constraint on the sum of variables
\sum x_{i}
associated with each region.
Thus, the regions
\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{g}}
can be thought of as identifying the geometry of the cut space
M_{\mathfrak{g}}
defined in (
2.39
), which is obtained by setting all of the tube polynomials in any
\mathsf{c}\in{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}
to zero.
More explicitly,

\displaystyle M_{\mathfrak{g}}=\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{|\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{g}}|})\in\mathbb{C}^{|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{G}}|}\setminus\mathcal{T}\,\big|\,\partial r_{1}=\cdots=\partial r_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{g}}}=0\}\,,

(2.47)

where
\partial r_{j}
represents the boundary of region
r_{j}
, as illustrated in (
2.46
).
The cut spaces
M_{\mathfrak{g}}
provide us with important topological building blocks when constructing the FRW homology, as we will see in sections
4.1
and
4.4
.

In the cut space
M_{\mathfrak{g}}
, the twisted variety
\mathcal{T}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{g}}
carves out a single bounded chamber denoted by
\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}
.
In the example graph from (
2.43
), this chamber is given by

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}:=\{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}_{-}:\partial\mathsf{r}_{123}=0\}\times\{x_{4}\in\mathbb{R}_{-}:\partial\mathsf{r}_{4}=0\}\times\{(x_{5},x_{6})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{-}:\partial\mathsf{r}_{56}=0\}\,,

(2.48)

and is illustrated in figure
1
.
Here, each piece of the Cartesian product corresponds to a region, and constitutes a 2-, 1- or 0-simplex in the corresponding negative orthant.

Figure 1
:
Left: The unique bounded chamber
\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}
on
M_{\mathfrak{g}}
expressed as a Cartesian product of region simplices.
Right: Plot of the hyperplanes
x_{1}=x_{2}=x_{6}=0
,

x_{3}=-f_{\mathsf{r}_{1}}-x_{1}-x_{2}=0
,
and
x_{5}=-f_{\mathsf{r}_{3}}-x_{6}=0
, where we have eliminated
x_{3}
,
x_{4}
, and
x_{5}
using (
2.46
).
Note that the on-shell conditions fix
x_{4}=-f_{\mathsf{r}_{2}}
, and therefore the twisted plane
x_{4}=0
does not appear in the right plot.
The bounded chamber corresponds to the solid orange bounded region.

This construction of graph regions generalizes straightforwardly to any acyclic decorated graph
{\mathfrak{g}}
, and shows that the on-shell conditions do not depend on the details of individual cut tubings
\mathsf{c}
.

\{X_{\bullet},Y_{\bullet}\}

\partial\mathsf{r}_{i}=0

}}}}}}$}}
are the broken, incoming and outgoing edges attached to the vertices of
\mathsf{r}
.
In this way, the regions determine the
letters

f_{\mathsf{r}}
that appear in the differential equation
57
.

In summary, the regions of
{\mathfrak{g}}
partition the vertices of
\mathcal{G}
.
Using this partition of vertices, we decompose the space
(x_{1},\cdots,x_{|\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{g}}|})\in\mathbb{C}^{|\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{g}}|}
into a Cartesian product of the spaces associated with each region
\mathsf{r}
, namely
\mathbb{C}_{\mathsf{r}}:=\{(x_{v_{1}},x_{v_{2}},\dots,x_{v_{|\mathsf{r}}|})\in\mathbb{C}^{|\mathsf{r}|}:v_{i=1,\dots,|\mathsf{r}|}\in\mathsf{r}\}
.
The on-shell conditions take the form
\{\partial\mathsf{r}=0:\mathsf{r}\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{g}}\}
, as in equation (
2.49
),
and each hyperplane
\partial\mathsf{r}=0
bounds an
(|\mathsf{r}|{-}1)
-dimensional simplex in the negative orthant of the region space
\mathbb{C}_{\mathsf{r}}
(for physical kinematics
X_{\bullet},Y_{\bullet}\geq 0
).
We denote the corresponding simplex by
\Delta_{\mathsf{r}}
.
Taking the Cartesian product of these region simplices produces the bounded chamber on the cut
M_{\mathfrak{g}}
:

\displaystyle\text{${\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}}:=$ ${\bigtimes}_{\mathsf{r}\in\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\Delta_{\mathsf{r}}$}

(2.50)

The bounded chamber constructed in this way is guaranteed to be a polytope, and therefore, a positive geometry (since all polytopes are positive geometries).
All physical (co)cycles are constructed starting from these positive geometries.

2.4
The two-chain (part 1): a pedagogical running example

The two-site chain,

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}{}}{}}}{{
