Indeed, we see that we can construct four independent integration contours in this way. One can verify that
|\chi\big(\mathbb{C}^{2}\setminus(\mathcal{T}\cup\mathcal{B})\big)|=4
, so we have indeed spanned the full space of twisted homology.

4.2
A basis of the dual relative twisted homology

Next, we introduce the (Poincaré) dual twisted homology
\check{H}_{n}^{\text{phys}}\subset H_{n}(M,\mathcal{B};\check{\mathcal{L}})
, where
\check{\mathcal{L}}:=\mathbb{C}\check{u}_{\mathcal{G}}
is the local system of the inverse twist
\check{u}_{\mathcal{G}}=u^{-1}_{\mathcal{G}}
.
There are two reasons for introducing the dual relative twisted homology:

1)

We want to use positive geometry to build the space of differential forms on
M\setminus\mathcal{B}
. Since the canonical form is a map from the relative twisted homology of the pair
(M,\mathcal{B})
to the space of differential forms on
M\setminus\mathcal{B}
, we construct
\check{H}_{n}^{\text{phys}}
.

2)

It is also useful to make sure that the homology intersection matrix is diagonal. This ensures that the cohomology intersection matrix of canonical forms—which appears in the coaction—is diagonal.

The dual relative twisted homology—generalities.

Let
C_{p}(M,\mathcal{B};\check{\mathcal{L}})
be the space of relative twisted chains
12
12

12

More precisely, we interpret all chains as locally-finite/Borel-Moore.
Since the locally-finite twisted homology is isomorphic to the compactly supported twisted homology we do not make the distinction between the two unless it is necessary.
We only provide a working definition aimed at applications in cosmology.

\displaystyle C_{p}(M,\mathcal{B};\check{\mathcal{L}}):=\{\check{\gamma}=\check{\Gamma}\otimes\check{u}_{\mathcal{G}}:\partial\check{\Gamma}\subset\mathcal{T}\cup\mathcal{B}\}\,.

(4.16)

Here,
\check{\Gamma}
is essentially the usual relative
p
-chain with boundaries on
\mathcal{T}\cup\mathcal{B}
.
The dual twisted boundary operator
\partial_{\check{u}}
works exactly like
\partial_{u}
.
In particular, the boundaries of
\check{\Gamma}
on any component of
\mathcal{T}
are in the kernel of the twisted boundary operator.
The relative twisted homology

\displaystyle H_{n}(M,\mathcal{B};\check{\mathcal{L}}):=\frac{\ker\partial_{\check{u}}:C_{n}(M,\mathcal{B};\check{\mathcal{L}})\to C_{n-1}(M,\mathcal{B};\check{\mathcal{L}})}{\mathrm{im}\partial_{\check{u}}:C_{n+1}(M,\mathcal{B};\check{\mathcal{L}})\to C_{n}(M,\mathcal{B};\check{\mathcal{L}})}

(4.17)

is the space of relative twisted
n
-chains with boundaries on
\mathcal{B}
that are not themselves a boundary of a relative twisted
(n+1)
-chain.
Like the partial twisted homology of section
4.1
, the relative twisted homology is also middle dimensional; only
n={|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{G}}|}
is non-trivial.

The physical subspace of relative twisted cycles from
{\mathfrak{g}}
.

The space of physical dual cycles is spanned by the set of relative twisted
{|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{G}}|}
-cycles whose maximal boundary corresponds to the bounded chamber
\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}
on the cut space
M_{\mathfrak{g}}
.
To build such dual cycles, we start with the
\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}
and let it grow into the negative orthant until it touches the hyperplane at infinity.
Explicitly,

\displaystyle\check{\Gamma}_{\mathfrak{g}}:=\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}:\begin{subarray}{c}B_{\tau}\leq 0\;\forall\;\tau\in\mathsf{c}\text{ and }\mathsf{c}\in{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}},\\
x_{v}\leq 0\;\forall\;v\in\mathsf{r}\text{ and }\mathsf{r}\in\mathsf{R}\text{ such that }|\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{r}}|\geq 2\end{subarray}\}\,.

(4.18)

This unbounded chamber has
\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}
on its maximal boundary

\displaystyle\partial_{{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\check{\Gamma}_{\mathfrak{g}}=|{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}|\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}\,,

(4.19)

where
\partial_{{\mathsf{C}}_{{\mathfrak{g}}}}:=\sum_{\mathsf{c}\in{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\sgn_{\mathsf{c}}\partial_{\mathsf{c}}
,
\partial_{\mathsf{c}}:=\partial_{\tau_{|\mathsf{c}|}}\circ\cdots\circ\partial_{\tau_{1}}
with
\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{|\mathsf{c}|}\in\mathsf{c}
and the
\tau_{i}
are ordered as usual according to box
2.1
.
Here,
\partial_{\tau}\bullet:=(\partial_{\check{u}}\bullet)\cap\mathcal{B}_{\tau}
is the part of the twisted boundary that lies inside the boundary
\mathcal{B}_{\tau}
.

Example: the dual homology of the two-site chain.

Applying (
4.18
), straightforwardly produces the relative twisted cycles
\check{\gamma}_{\mathfrak{g}}=\check{\Gamma}_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes\check{u}_{\mathcal{G}}
where

\displaystyle\check{\Gamma}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/2Chain_gammaDual4.pdf}\,,

\displaystyle\check{\Gamma}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/2Chain_gammaDual2.pdf}\,,

(4.20)

\displaystyle\check{\Gamma}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gl}}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/2Chain_gammaDual3.pdf}\,,

\displaystyle\check{\Gamma}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gb}}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/2Chain_gammaDual1.pdf}\,.

These cycles are dual to the basis constructed in section
4.1
via the homology intersection number.
Since the intersection number of twisted cycles is proportional to the usual topological intersection number, and, because the topological cycles
\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}(\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}})
and
\check{\Gamma}_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}
share support (on
\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}})
if and only if
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}
, the resulting intersection matrix must be diagonal.

By ensuring that the dual contours have a diagonal intersection matrix with the physical FRW-cycles, the canonical forms of the dual cycles (
\Omega[\check{\Gamma}_{\mathfrak{g}}]
) pair nicely with the physical FRW-cycles.
To leading order in the parameters
\alpha_{\bullet}
, the resulting period matrix is diagonal.

4.3
Positive geometry: from dual homology to FRW cohomology

In this section, we provide an explicit representation for the canonical form map which generates our basis of FRW forms, and review some basis aspects of twisted cohomology.

Partially twisted cohomology—generalities.

To work at the level of cohomology, we quotient by total derivatives since these integrals vanish.
Explicitly, for any
n
-form
\varphi
on
M\setminus\mathcal{B}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\gamma}\mathrm{d}(u_{\mathcal{G}}\;\varphi)=\int_{\gamma}u_{\mathcal{G}}\;(\nabla_{\omega}\varphi)=0\quad\forall\quad\gamma\in H_{n}(M\setminus\mathcal{B};\mathcal{L})

(4.21)

where

\displaystyle\nabla_{\omega}=\mathrm{d}+\omega\wedge\,,\qquad\omega:=\mathrm{dlog}\;u_{\mathcal{G}}\,,

(4.22)

is the twisted differential.
That is, the integral only depends on the equivalence class of
\varphi
:
\varphi\simeq\varphi+\nabla_{\omega}\psi
for any
\varphi\in\Omega^{n}(M\setminus\mathcal{B})
and
\psi\in\Omega^{n-1}(M\setminus\mathcal{B})
where
\Omega^{n}(M\setminus\mathcal{B})
is the set of
n
-forms on
M\setminus\mathcal{B}
.
The partial twisted cohomology is the set of equivalence classes

\displaystyle H^{p}(M\setminus\mathcal{B};\nabla_{\omega})

\displaystyle=\frac{\ker\left[\nabla_{\omega}:\Omega^{p}(M\setminus\mathcal{B})\to\Omega^{p+1}(M\setminus\mathcal{B})\right]}{\mathrm{im}\left[\nabla_{\omega}:\Omega^{p-1}(M\setminus\mathcal{B})\to\Omega^{p}(M\setminus\mathcal{B})\right]}\,,

(4.23)

\displaystyle=\frac{\{\varphi\in\Omega^{p}(M\setminus\mathcal{B}):\nabla_{\omega}\varphi=0\}}{\{\nabla_{\omega}\psi:\psi\in\Omega^{p-1}(M\setminus\mathcal{B})\}}\,.

The physical subspace of partially twisted cocycles from
{\mathfrak{g}}
.

In practice, because the underlying geometry (here, a hyperplane arrangement) is a positive geometry, the partial twisted cohomology can be simply generated by the
canonical form
map
27
;
9

13
13

13

Mathematically, there are still some unresolved subtleties with this map in the context of twisted (co)homology.
While the locally-finite twisted homology is isomorphic to the compactly-supported twisted homology, the space of locally-finite twisted chains is much larger than the space of compactly-supported twisted chains.
In fact, the space of locally-finite twisted chains contains the space of compactly-supported twisted chains.
At the time of writing, the canonical form map is only well-defined for locally-finite twisted chains that are “maximally” locally-finite (see
26
).
In practice, we find that we span the space of the relative twisted homology with maximally locally-finite chains for which the canonical form map makes sense, so we ignore possible pathologies.

\displaystyle{\Omega}:H_{|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{G}}|}(M,\mathcal{B};\check{\mathcal{L}})\to H^{|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{G}}|}(M\setminus\mathcal{B};\nabla_{\omega})\,.

(4.24)

Restricting to the space of physical dual cycles
\check{\Gamma}_{\mathfrak{g}}
produces the physical subspace of differential forms
\varphi_{\mathfrak{g}}:=\Omega[\check{\Gamma}_{\mathfrak{g}}]
with logarithmic singularities on
\partial\check{\Gamma}_{\mathfrak{g}}
.
An explicit representation for
\varphi_{\mathfrak{g}}
was provided in equation (
2.81
).

Example: canonical forms of the two-site chain.

The cocycles
\varphi_{\mathfrak{g}}
for the two-site chain were quoted without derivation in (
2.65
). Here we recover them from the general construction (
2.81
), which expresses each
\varphi_{\mathfrak{g}}
as the canonical form of the dual unbounded chamber
\check{\Gamma}_{\mathfrak{g}}
built in section
4.2
.

Using equation (
2.81
), the canonical forms associated to our basis of dual cycles are

\displaystyle\Omega[\check{\Gamma}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chainB12}}\wedge\tilde{\Omega}[\Delta_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}]=\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chainB12}}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}\frac{x_{2}}{x_{1}}\,,

(4.25)

\displaystyle\Omega[\check{\Gamma}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}]

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chainB12}}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chainB1}}\,,

\displaystyle\Omega[\check{\Gamma}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gl}}]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chainB12}}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chainB2}}\,,

\displaystyle\Omega[\check{\Gamma}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gb}}]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chainB1}}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chainB2}}\,.

The extra sign in
\Omega[\check{\Gamma}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}]
is crucial to make the combinatorics of the signs in the kinematic connection simple
57
.
It is easy to verify that these differential forms are indeed canonical forms of the bounded regions in (
4.2
).
It is also easy to verify that the maximal cuts of these forms are the canonical form of
\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}
.
For example,

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/2ChainB12.pdf}}\left[\Omega[\check{\Gamma}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}]\right]

\displaystyle=\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/2ChainB12.pdf}}\left[\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chainB12}}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}\frac{x_{2}}{x_{1}}\right]=\mathrm{dlog}\frac{x_{2}|_{B_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chainB12}}=0}}{x_{1}|_{B_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chainB12}}=0}}=\Omega[\Delta_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}]\,,

(4.26)

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/2ChainB1B2.pdf}}\left[\Omega[\check{\Gamma}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gb}}]\right]

\displaystyle=\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/2ChainB1B2.pdf}}\left[\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/2ChainB1.pdf}}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/2ChainB2.pdf}}\right]=1=\Omega[\Delta_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gb}}]\,,

where
\Delta_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}
is a line segment and
\Delta_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gb}}
is a point.

4.4
Positive geometry: from the FRW-homology to dual cohomology

In section
4.2
, we introduced the dual relative twisted homology and described a basis of physical cycles.
These cycles can be paired via integration with dual cocycles—elements of the dual relative twisted cohomology
\check{H}^{n}_{\mathrm{phys}}:=H^{n}(M,\mathcal{B};\nabla_{-\omega})
—to form dual periods.
Furthermore, the dual relative twisted cohomology can be paired with the FRW cohomology via intersection numbers to form an inner product on these vector spaces; we encountered these intersection numbers in (
3.18
).
Like the FRW homology, the dual relative twisted cohomology can be geometrically decomposed into simple building blocks, each of which can be naturally associated with a physical cut.

The dual relative twisted cohomology and the physical subspace.

More formally, the dual cohomology we are discussing is the Poincaré dual of the FRW-homology. There exists a
dual canonical form
, which maps a cut contour to a dual form in the relative twisted cohomology

\displaystyle{\check{\Omega}}:H_{|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{G}}|}(M\setminus\mathcal{B};\check{\mathcal{L}})\to H^{|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{G}}|}(M,\mathcal{B};\nabla_{-\omega})\,,\quad\text{via}\quad\delta_{{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}}(\Gamma_{\mathfrak{g}})\mapsto\delta^{*}_{{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}}(\Omega[\Gamma_{\mathfrak{g}}])\,,

(4.27)

where

\displaystyle\delta_{{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}}^{*}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{c}\in{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\sgn_{\mathsf{c}}\delta_{\mathsf{c}}\,,\qquad\delta_{\mathsf{c}}^{*}=\delta_{\tau_{|\mathsf{c}|}}\circ\cdots\circ\delta_{\tau_{1}}\,,\qquad\mathsf{c}=(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{|\mathsf{c}|})\,,

(4.28)

and
\delta^{*}_{\tau}
is the dual coboundary symbol.
Similar to the coboundary map, the dual coboundary maps a form on a cut to a form in the larger ambient topological space in which the cut is embedded.

Intuitively, one can think of
\delta_{\tau}^{*}(\check{\phi})
as extending the support of the lower-degree form
\check{\phi}
which is defined on the cut
M_{\tau}
into an infinitesimal tubular neighborhood around the subvariety
\mathcal{B}_{\tau}
.
14
14

14

More precisely,
\delta_{\tau}^{*}
is a map

\delta_{\tau}^{*}:H^{n-1}((M\setminus\mathcal{B})\cap\mathcal{B}_{\tau};\nabla_{-\omega}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\tau}})\to H^{n-1}(M\setminus\mathcal{B};\nabla_{-\omega})
and
\delta_{\mathsf{c}}^{*}
is the iteration of this map.
See
80
for a construction of the coboundary and its dual in the untwisted context.
The generalization to the twisted context can be found in
37
or
38
.

While one can make
\delta_{\mathsf{c}}^{*}
explicit as a differential form using bump functions,
15
15

15

More explicitly, the iterated dual coboundary can be represented as

\delta_{\mathsf{c}}^{*}:=\frac{\check{u}}{\check{u}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{c}}}}\bigwedge_{\tau\in\mathsf{c}}\mathrm{d}\theta_{\tau}

where
\theta_{\tau}=\theta(|B_{\tau}|>\epsilon)
is the Heaviside step function (which can be replaced by a smooth bump function) that is unity in an infinitesimal neighborhood
|B_{\tau}|<\epsilon\ll 1
.

we will only need the following algebraic properties of the dual coboundary:

1)

Multi-index notation
:

\displaystyle\delta^{*}_{\mathsf{c}^{\prime}}\circ\delta^{*}_{\mathsf{c}}:=\delta^{*}_{\mathsf{c},\mathsf{c}^{\prime}}\,.

(4.29)

2)

Anti-symmetry
: whenever the multi-index
{\mathsf{C}}=(\mathsf{c}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{c}_{k})
corresponds to a generic normal-crossing component
\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{C}}
in the on-shell variety, we have

\displaystyle\delta^{*}_{{\mathsf{C}}}:=\sgn(\sigma)\delta^{*}_{\sigma({\mathsf{C}})}

(4.30)

where
\sigma
is any permutation of
{\mathsf{C}}
.

3)

Identities
: the coboundary operators satisfy the same identities as
\delta_{\mathsf{C}}
and
\text{Res}_{\mathsf{C}}
when the multi-index
{\mathsf{C}}
corresponds to a component
\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{C}}
of the on-shell variety that is
not
normal-crossing.

4)

Differentiation
: upon differentiation, the coboundary gives rise to boundary terms, namely

\displaystyle\nabla_{-\omega}\delta^{*}_{\mathsf{c}}(\phi):=(-1)^{|\mathsf{c}|}\left[\delta^{*}_{\mathsf{c}}\Big((\nabla_{-\omega}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{c}}})\phi\Big)-\sum_{\tau\notin\mathsf{c}}\delta^{*}_{\mathsf{c}\tau}(\phi|_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{c}\tau}})\right]\,.

(4.31)

These boundary terms are lower degree forms on cuts that can appear when using Stokes theorem (we make this explicit below).

Like the partially twisted homology, the relative twisted cohomology has a direct sum decomposition.
While this is a property of the full relative twisted cohomology, we only exhibit this decomposition for the physical subspace:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \check{H}^{|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{G}}|}_{\text{phys}}:=\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{g}}\;\delta_{{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}}^{*}\Big(H^{{|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{G}}|}-|\mathsf{c}|}(M_{\mathfrak{g}};\nabla_{-\omega}|_{\mathcal{B}_{{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}}})\Big)\subseteq H^{|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{G}}|}(M,\mathcal{B};\nabla_{-\omega})\,,\end{aligned}

(4.32)

where
\delta^{*}_{{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}}:=\sum_{\mathsf{c}\in{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\sgn_{\mathsf{c}}\delta_{\mathsf{c}}^{*}
like in (
4.11
).
The relative twisted homology characterizes the “interesting” integrands on each cut
M_{\mathfrak{g}}
—including those produced using Stokes theorem.
Then, since each
M_{\mathfrak{g}}
is a positive geometry (with a single bounded chamber
\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}
), the twisted homology is built from the direct sum of twisted cohomologies of positive geometries.

Not surprisingly, we can pair dual cohomology classes with dual homology classes to construct dual periods

\displaystyle\int_{\check{\gamma}}\check{u}\;\delta^{*}_{\mathsf{c}}(\varphi)=\int_{\partial_{\mathsf{c}}\check{\gamma}}\check{u}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{c}}}\;\varphi

(4.33)

where
\varphi\in H^{n-|\mathsf{c}|}(M_{\mathsf{c}};\nabla_{-\omega}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{c}}})
, and
\partial_{\mathsf{c}}\check{\gamma}\in H_{n-|\mathsf{c}|}(M_{\mathsf{c}};\check{\mathcal{L}}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{c}}})
are (co)cycles on the cut
M_{\mathsf{c}}
.
Inside an integral, the dual coboundary induces a boundary on the dual contour such that the integral pairs a contour and form on the same cut (topological space).

Moreover, the boundary terms produced by differentiation of the dual coboundary ensure that exact dual forms vanish after integration:
\int_{\check{\gamma}}\check{u}\;\nabla_{-\omega}\delta^{*}_{\mathsf{c}}(\phi)=0
.
Explicitly,

\displaystyle\int_{\check{\gamma}}\check{u}\;\nabla_{-\omega}\delta^{*}_{\mathsf{c}}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\check{\gamma}}\check{u}\;(-1)^{|\mathsf{c}|}\left[\delta^{*}_{\mathsf{c}}\Big((\nabla_{-\omega}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{c}}})\phi\Big)-\sum_{\tau\notin\mathsf{c}}\delta^{*}_{\mathsf{c}\tau}(\phi|_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{c}\tau}})\right]\,,

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\mathsf{c}|}\left[\int_{\partial_{\mathsf{c}}\check{\gamma}}(\check{u}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{c}}})\;(\nabla_{-\omega}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{c}}})\phi-\sum_{\tau\notin\mathsf{c}}\int_{\partial_{\mathsf{c}\tau}\check{\gamma}}(\check{u}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{c}\tau}})\;(\phi|_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{c}\tau}})\right]\,,

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\mathsf{c}|}\left[\int_{\partial(\partial_{\mathsf{c}}\check{\gamma})}(\check{u}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{c}}})\;\phi-\sum_{\tau\notin\mathsf{c}}\int_{\partial_{\mathsf{c}\tau}\check{\gamma}}(\check{u}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{c}\tau}})\;(\phi|_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{c}\tau}})\right]\,,

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\mathsf{c}|}\sum_{\tau\notin\mathsf{c}}\left[\int_{\partial_{\tau}\partial_{\mathsf{c}}\check{\gamma}}(\check{u}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{c}}\tau})\;(\phi|_{\mathcal{B}_{\tau}})-\int_{\partial_{\mathsf{c}\tau}\check{\gamma}}(\check{u}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{c}\tau}})\;(\phi|_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{c}\tau}})\right]=0\,.

(4.34)

Here, we have used (
4.31
) in the first line, and equation (
4.33
) in the second line.
In the third line, we have decomposed the boundary operator into the parts that project onto one boundary component of
\mathcal{B}
:
\partial(\partial_{\mathsf{c}}\check{\gamma})=\sum_{\tau\notin\mathsf{c}}\partial_{\tau}\partial\mathsf{c}\check{\gamma}
.
Finally, in the last line we have used the identification
\partial_{\tau}\circ\partial_{\mathsf{c}}:=\partial_{\mathsf{c}\tau}
.
Therefore, as usual, we can add the total derivative of any dual form without changing the value of the original integral.

While we have only defined a subspace of the relative twisted cohomology indirectly through the association (
4.27
), in general, it is the space of dual forms that are closed and not themselves total derivatives.

Example: the canonical dual forms of the two-site chain.

Here, we build the dual cocycles
\check{\varphi}_{\mathfrak{g}}
as canonical forms of the FRW cycles
\gamma_{\mathfrak{g}}
from section
2.4
.
These supply one factor of the cohomology intersection pairing
\langle\check{\varphi}_{\mathfrak{g}}|\varphi_{\mathfrak{h}}\rangle
used in the master coaction formula (
3.12
).

Relative cohomology tracks the boundary terms that arise from Stokes’ theorem when the integration contours end on the untwisted hyperplanes
\mathcal{B}_{\tau}
. For the two-site chain, the twisted variety
\mathcal{B}
has three components,

\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/2ChainB1.pdf}}\cup\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/2ChainB2.pdf}}\cup\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/2ChainB12.pdf}}

where, as usual,
\mathcal{B}_{\tau}=V(B_{\tau})
and the
B_{\tau}
’s for the two-site chain graph are given in (
2.53
).
The on-shell variety was depicted in equation (
2.54
), while the full hyperplane arrangement along with its cut spaces was depicted in figure
4
.
From (
2.60
) we have

\displaystyle\check{\varphi}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}

\displaystyle=\delta^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chainCs.pdf}}\left(\Omega\left[\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/2chainM12.pdf}\right]\right)=\delta^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chainCs.pdf}}\left(\mathrm{dlog}\frac{x_{2}}{x_{1}}\Big|_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chainCs.pdf}}\right)=\delta^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2ChainB12.pdf}}\left(\mathrm{dlog}\frac{x_{2}}{-x_{2}-X_{1}-X_{2}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\check{\varphi}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}

\displaystyle=\delta^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chainCr.pdf}}\left(\Omega\left[\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2ChainM12_1.pdf}\right]\right)=\delta^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chainCr.pdf}}\left(1\right)=\delta^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chainB12.pdf},\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chainB1.pdf},}\left(1\right)\,,

\displaystyle\check{\varphi}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gl}}

\displaystyle=\delta^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chainCr.pdf}}\left(\Omega\left[\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2ChainM12_2.pdf}\right]\right)=\delta^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chainCl.pdf}}\left(1\right)=\delta^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chainB12.pdf},\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chainB2.pdf}}\left(1\right)\,,

\displaystyle\check{\varphi}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gb}}

\displaystyle=\delta^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chainCr.pdf}}\left(\Omega\left[\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2ChainM1_2.pdf}\right]\right)=\delta^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chainCb.pdf}}\left(1\right)=\delta^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chainB1.pdf},\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chainB2.pdf},}\left(1\right)\,,

(4.35)

where, in the first line above, we chose to eliminate
x_{1}
when restricting to the loci
B_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2ChainB12.pdf}}=0
.

Each form in (
4.4
) generates the twisted cohomology of its corresponding cut and exemplifies the decomposition (
4.32
):

\displaystyle\check{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{phys}}

\displaystyle=H^{2}\left(\mathbb{C}^{2}\setminus\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/2ChainVarT.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/2ChainVarB.pdf};\nabla_{-\omega}\right)

(4.36)

\displaystyle=\delta^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2ChainB12}}\circ H^{1}\left(\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2ChainM12};\nabla_{-\omega}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2ChainB12}}}\right)

\displaystyle\oplus\delta^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2ChainCb}}\circ H^{0}\left(\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2ChainM1_2}\right)\oplus\delta^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2ChainCr}}\circ H^{0}\left(\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2ChainM12_1}\right)\oplus\delta^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2ChainCl}}\circ H^{0}\left(\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2ChainM12_2}\right)\,.

Note that
\omega
vanishes when restricted to a point.
Therefore, the dual covariant derivative reduces to the usual exterior derivative on a point
\nabla_{-\omega}|_{\mathrm{point}}=\mathrm{d}
.
The two-site chain is also a special case in which
H^{2}_{\mathrm{phys}}
is equal to the full relative twisted cohomology.

4.5
The intersection number as an inner product

In this section, we provide a lightning review of intersection theory and the importance of the (dual) canonical forms for formulating the coaction.
A pictorial summary of these building blocks and how they are related to each other was already presented in figure
3
.

The (co)homology intersection number is a non-degenerate pairing between elements of the FRW (co)homology and dual (co)homology

\displaystyle\langle\check{\varphi}|\varphi\rangle:

\displaystyle H^{|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{G}}|}(M,\mathcal{B};\nabla_{-\omega})\times H^{|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{G}}|}(M\setminus\mathcal{B};\nabla_{\omega})\to\mathbb{C}[[\alpha_{\bullet}^{\pm},X_{\bullet}^{\pm},Y_{\bullet}^{\pm}]]\,,

(4.37)

\displaystyle[\gamma|\check{\gamma}]:

\displaystyle H_{|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{G}}|}(M\setminus\mathcal{B};\mathcal{L})\times H_{|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{G}}|}(M,\mathcal{B};\check{\mathcal{L}})\to\mathbb{C}[[e^{\pm i\pi\alpha_{\bullet}},(1-e^{\pm i\pi\alpha_{\bullet}})]]\,.

(4.38)

Here,
\mathbb{C}[[x,y,\dots]]
denotes the space of Laurent polynomials functions in the variables
\{x,y,\dots\}
with
\mathbb{C}
-coefficients.
These pairings can be used like an inner product to project any element of the (co)homology onto a chosen basis.
Moreover, we have the following resolution of identity

\displaystyle\mathds{1}

\displaystyle=|\varphi_{a}\rangle C_{ab}^{-1}\langle\check{\varphi}_{b}|\,,

(4.39)

where
\{\varphi_{a}\}
and
\{\check{\varphi}_{a}\}

constitute bases of the cohomology and dual cohomology,
and
C_{ab}=\langle\check{\varphi}_{a}|\varphi_{b}\rangle
is
the (co)homology intersection matrix.

In the setting of partially twisted FRW hyperplane arrangements and logarithmic differential forms, the cohomology intersection number simplifies to a specific sequential residue built from the data of a dual form.
For any cut
\mathsf{c}
(possibly not physical) in the on-shell variety and any bounded region
\Delta_{\mathsf{c}}
on
M_{\mathsf{c}}
, one can define the dual form
\delta^{*}_{\mathsf{c}}(\Omega[\Delta_{\mathsf{c}}])
.
In the intersection number, this dual-form induces the following action on any FRW-form
\varphi

\displaystyle\langle\delta_{\mathsf{c}}^{*}(\Omega[\Delta_{\mathsf{c}}])|\varphi\rangle=\sum_{\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{V}_{\Delta_{\mathsf{c}}}}\frac{1}{\prod_{i}^{|\mathbf{w}|}\alpha_{w_{i}}}\text{Res}_{x_{w_{1}}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{c}}}=0}\circ\cdots\circ\text{Res}_{x_{w_{|\mathbf{w}|}}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{c}}}=0}\circ\text{Res}_{\mathsf{c}}[\varphi]\,.

(4.40)

Here,
\mathcal{V}_{\Delta_{\mathsf{c}}}
is the set of all vertices of the bounded region
\Delta_{\mathsf{c}}
; each element,
\mathbf{w}
, of this set indexes which twisted hyperplanes meet at a given vertex.
Equation (
4.40
) is enough to prove all results following from intersection theory in this work; there are corrections when
\varphi
or the argument of
\delta_{\mathsf{c}}^{*}
have higher order poles.
In such situations, see
37
;
38
;
75
for more details.
Using (
4.40
) and the basis constructed in sections
4.3
and
4.4
, the intersection number between physical forms is

\displaystyle C_{{\mathfrak{g}}{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}:=\langle\check{\varphi}_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}}|\varphi_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}}\rangle=\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}\;|{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}|\;\prod_{\mathsf{r}\in\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\frac{\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{r}}}\alpha_{v}}{\prod_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{r}}}\alpha_{v}}

(4.41)

where
\delta_{{\mathfrak{g}}{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}
is the Kronecker delta symbol and
|{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}|
is the cardinality of the set
{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}
.

4.6
Partial fractions, zonotopes and the flow of cuts/derivatives

In this section, we review the partial fractions decomposition of the physical FRW-form
\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}
into the cut basis of section
4.1
.
The partial-fractioned expression for
\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}
manifests the claims about the independent physical residues in sections
2.1
and
2.2
.
It also leads to the identification of
graphical zonotopes
in the on-shell variety.
These zonotopes encode the combinatorics of the sequential residues and the differential equations of the physical subspace.
These combinatorics will be used to simplify sums in the coaction.

\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{E}}(\mathcal{G})
be the set of acyclic minors with fixed broken edge set
\mathcal{E}
and

be the subset of

with no pinched edges

(4.42)

where

and
\mathcal{E}^{\!\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{
\hbox to14.58pt{\vbox to3.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.6pt\lower-1.6pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}

{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 2.21 0 C 2.21 1.22 1.22 2.21 0 2.21 C -1.22 2.21 -2.21 1.22 -2.21 0 C -2.21 -1.22 -1.22 -2.21 0 -2.21 C 1.22 -2.21 2.21 -1.22 2.21 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

}}}}}}$}}_{\mathfrak{g}}
denote the set of broken and pinched edges of the minor
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
Then, the physical wavefunction has the following alternative expression that is fully partial fractioned in the
x_{i}
variables
57
:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{E}\subset\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{G}}}(-1)^{|\mathcal{E}|}\sum_{{\mathfrak{g}}\in\mathcal{A}^{\varnothing}_{\mathcal{E}}(\mathcal{G})}\varphi_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\,.

(4.43)

For example, consider the three-site chain.
In the language of (
4.43
), the physical FRW-form is

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain.pdf}}=&\left(\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rr.pdf}}+\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rl.pdf}}+\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ll.pdf}}+\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_lr.pdf}}\right)\\
&-\left(\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_br.pdf}}+\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_bl.pdf}}\right)\\
&-\left(\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rb.pdf}}+\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_lb.pdf}}\right)\\
&+\left(\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_bb.pdf}}\right)\,.\end{aligned}

(4.44)

With the exception of one acyclic minor, each
{\mathfrak{g}}
corresponds to a unique set of cut-tubings
{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}
.
Thus, most
\mathrm{dlog}{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}
are a single term:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rr.pdf}}&=\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT1.pdf}}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT12.pdf}}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT123.pdf}}\,,\\
\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_bl.pdf}}&=\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT1.pdf}}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT23.pdf}}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT3.pdf}}\,,\\
\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_bb.pdf}}&=\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT1.pdf}}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT2.pdf}}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT3.pdf}}\,.\end{aligned}

(4.45)

The
\mathrm{dlog}
’s that correspond to a degenerate cut are captured by a single linear combination:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_lr.pdf}}&=\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT12.pdf}}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT2.pdf}}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT123.pdf}}\\
&\quad+\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT123.pdf}}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT2.pdf}}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainT23.pdf}}\,.\end{aligned}

(4.46)

There is a strong sense in which (
4.43
) better represents the structure of FRW cosmological integrals than (
2.7
).
It manifests the fact that each cut
{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}
contributes a specific combination of
\mathrm{dlog}
’s as claimed in sections
2.1
and
2.2
.

:
The on-shell variety restricted to the total energy plane of the three-site chain graph
(
\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/3chainT123}}
, not the cut space

M_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf}}=\mathcal{T}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/3chainT123}}
).
The
cyan
and

\mathcal{B}_{\tau}
.
The points in
magenta

,

,
and,

;
all of these appear in (
4.47
).
Note that
corresponds to the intersection of four propagators and is a degenerate point of the on-shell variety.
One way to resolve this degeneracy is to move the
gray
line in the direction normal to itself (see right above).
After deforming the
gray
line, the canonical form of the
red
region gets contributions from five vertices.
In fact,
\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_lr.pdf}}
is the sum of two terms corresponding to the two vertices after resolving the degeneracy as depicted in the right figure above.

Zonotopes in the on-shell variety.

Here, we show that the residues of the physical FRW-form
\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}
are canonical forms of graphical zonotopes.
A zonotope is a convex polytope that is the Minkowski sum of finitely many line segments.
Some familiar three-dimensional examples are the triangular prism, cube, and rhombic dodecahedron.
The above three-dimensional examples are also examples of graphical zonotopes.
For graphical zonotopes, the line segments in the Minkowski sum correspond to the edges of
G
, encapsulating both combinatorial and geometric data.

To see how graphical zonotopes arise in our context, consider taking the
B_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf}}=0
residue of the form
\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain.pdf}}
:

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain.pdf}}]

\displaystyle=\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rr.pdf}}+\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rl.pdf}}+\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ll.pdf}}+\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_lr.pdf}}]\,.

(4.47)

The
B_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf}}=0
residue annihilates all
\varphi_{\mathfrak{g}}
for
{\mathfrak{g}}\notin\mathcal{A}^{\varnothing}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf}}(\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain.pdf})
.
Examining the surviving terms reveals that

\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain.pdf}}]
is a two-form that has singularities on the boundary of the
red
shaded rectangle (zonotope) labeled by
\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf}}
in figure
5
.
In fact, on the total energy cut, the physical form
\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain.pdf}}
is proportional to the canonical form of the zonotope
\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf}}

\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain.pdf}}]\propto\Omega[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf}}]
.

To verify this, we can compute all other residues
B_{\tau}=0
for
\tau\in\{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chainT1},\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chainT3},\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chainT12},\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chainT23}\}
. This yields the canonical forms of the boundaries of
\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf}}
:

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rs.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain.pdf}}]

\displaystyle=\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rs.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rr.pdf}}+\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rl.pdf}}]\propto\Omega\left[\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainZonoSquareR.pdf}\right]=:\Omega\left[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rs.pdf}}\right]\,,

(4.48)

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ls.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain.pdf}}]

\displaystyle=\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ls.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ll.pdf}}+\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_lr.pdf}}]\propto\Omega\left[\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainZonoSquareL.pdf}\right]=:\Omega\left[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ls.pdf}}\right]\,,

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_sr.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain.pdf}}]

\displaystyle=\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_sr.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rr.pdf}}+\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_lr.pdf}}]\propto\Omega\left[\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainZonoSquareT.pdf}\right]=:\Omega\left[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_sr.pdf}}\right]\,,

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_sl.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain.pdf}}]

\displaystyle=\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_sl.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rl.pdf}}+\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ll.pdf}}]\propto\Omega\left[\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainZonoSquareB.pdf}\right]=:\Omega\left[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_sl.pdf}}\right]\,.

Each of the above forms is a one-form on the
cyan
lines in figure
6
, and, is singular on the
magenta
points that lie on said line.
Finally, we take the maximal cuts to find that

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rr.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain.pdf}}]

\displaystyle=\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rr.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rr.pdf}}]\propto\Omega\left[\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainZonoSquareTR}\right]=:\Omega\left[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ll.pdf}}\right]\,,

(4.49)

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ll.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain.pdf}}]

\displaystyle=\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ll.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ll.pdf}}]\propto\Omega\left[\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainZonoSquareBL.pdf}\right]=:\Omega\left[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rr.pdf}}\right]\,,

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_lr.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain.pdf}}]

\displaystyle=\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_lr.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_lr.pdf}}]\propto\Omega\left[\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainZonoSquareTL.pdf}\right]=:\Omega\left[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_lr.pdf}}\right]\,,

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rl.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain.pdf}}]

\displaystyle=\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rl.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rl.pdf}}]\propto\Omega\left[\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chainZonoSquareBR.pdf}\right]=:\Omega\left[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rl.pdf}}\right]\,.

Since the residues in (
4.48
) and (
4.49
) are canonical forms of the codimension-one and -two boundaries of
\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf}}
,

\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain.pdf}}]

is indeed the canonical form of
\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf}}
.

:

The forms

\displaystyle=-\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_bs.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_br.pdf}}+\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_bl.pdf}}]\propto\Omega[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_sb.pdf}}]\,,

(4.50)

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_sb.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain.pdf}}]

\displaystyle=-\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_sb.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rb.pdf}}+\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_lb.pdf}}]\propto\Omega[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_sb.pdf}}]\,,

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_bb.pdf}}[\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain.pdf}}]

are proportional to the canonical forms of the line segments highlighted in

Box 4.2: Graphical zonotopes

To each decorated graph
\mathfrak{z}
with only broken (​​

​​) and pinched (​​

​​) edges we associate a
graphical zonotope

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{z}}
, defined as the unique polytope satisfying

\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{z}}[\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}]\;=\;\Omega[\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{z}}]\,,

whose canonical form is the corresponding residue of
\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}
.

Faces from anti-pinches.

Lower-dimensional faces of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{z}}
are themselves graphical zonotopes
\mathcal{Z}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{z}}}
, where
\tilde{\mathfrak{z}}
is an
anti-pinch
of
\mathfrak{z}
: an acyclic minor obtained by turning some pinched edges into oriented ones,

}}}}}}$}
. We write
\mathrm{antipinch}(\mathfrak{z})
for the set of all anti-pinches of
\mathfrak{z}
(including
\mathfrak{z}
itself), and
\mathrm{antipinch}_{n}(\mathfrak{z}):=\{\mathfrak{f}\in\mathrm{antipinch}(\mathfrak{z}):|\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{f}}|-|\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{z}}|=n\}
. The facets of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{z}}
are labeled by
\mathrm{antipinch}_{1}(\mathfrak{z})
.

The zonotope
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{z}}
embedded into the on-shell variety is obtained by taking the convex hull of its vertices.
The vertices of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{z}}
are labeled by the acyclic minors that descend from
\mathfrak{z}
and have no pinched edges (namely, all pinched edges of
\mathfrak{z}
are turned into oriented edges).
Let
\mathrm{antipinch}_{\mathrm{max}}(\mathfrak{z})

be the set of acyclic minors where all pinched edges have been replaced by all possible oriented edges that do not produce a cyclic graph; then

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{z}}=\mathrm{ConvHull}\Big(\{\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{u}\in\mathrm{antipinch}_{\mathrm{max}}(\mathfrak{z})}\}\Big)\,,

(4.51)

where we recall that
\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{u}}=\bigcap_{\tau\in\mathsf{c}}V(S_{\tau})
for any
\mathsf{c}\in{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{u}}
.
The convex hull definition is valid for any lower-dimension faces.
The only difference is that
\mathfrak{z}
starts with some oriented edges that are shared with all of its descendants
\tilde{\mathfrak{z}}
.

The flow of cuts and derivatives from zonotopes.

It is well known that the discontinuity of an integral can be computed as a linear combination of sequential residue contours (see
42
;
71
;
80
;
68
;
87
for a very incomplete survey).
A number of sequential discontinuities are known to annihilate the physical subspace, due to the Steinmann relations
84
;
85
;
35
;
34
;
16
;
19
;
67
and the hierarchical principle
21
;
74
;
68
;
66
.
These relations constrain the kind of functions FRW-integrals evaluate to.
In this work we have classified the set of physical sequential residues that do not annihilate the physical subspace.
This classification is of course consistent with the Steinmann relations and hierarchical principle, but is even more complete.

Following
58
, we can also formalize the combinatorics of the physical sequential residues in what is called the
flow of cuts
.
Through (
4.43
), the combinatorics of the physical sequential residues is inherited from the collection of zonotopes
\{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{z}}\}
,
since the combinatorics of the residues of
\Omega[\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{z}}]
is inherited from the combinatorial structure of the zonotope
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{z}}
:

Box 4.3: The flow of physical cuts

The cut tubing
{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}
and its associated residue operator
\text{Res}_{{\mathsf{C}}_{{\mathfrak{g}}}}
projects
H^{|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{G}}|}_{\text{phys}}
onto the subspace of physical forms that only have on-shell singularities compatible with the zonotope
\mathcal{Z}_{{\mathfrak{g}}}
.
In order to not leave the space
H^{|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{G}}|}_{\text{phys}}
, further compatible residues must localize to lower-dimensional faces of
\mathcal{Z}_{{\mathfrak{g}}}
:

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\to\text{Res}_{{\mathsf{C}}_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}}\quad\text{if}\quad{\mathfrak{g}}\in\mathrm{antipinch}_{1}({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime})\,.

(4.52)

As a consequence of the combinatorics of physical cuts, and because the cut-basis of FRW-forms is tailored to these combinatorics, the resulting differential equations have a block triangular form in which each block corresponds to a top-dimensional zonotope
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{z}}
where
\mathfrak{z}
has no oriented edges.
The kinematic derivative of
\varphi_{\mathfrak{g}}
couples to itself and all
\varphi_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}
such that
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{antipinch}_{1}({\mathfrak{g}})
.
Note that the flow (arrows between
{\mathfrak{g}}
and
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}
) is reversed compared to the flow of residues; if
\mathfrak{z}\in\mathrm{antipinch}({\mathfrak{g}})
corresponds to an
m
-dimensional face of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{z}}
, then
\mathfrak{z}\in\mathrm{antipinch}({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime})
corresponds to a compatible
(m{+}1)
-dimensional face of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{z}}
.
This is a natural consequence of the duality between discontinuities and derivatives.

5
Examples and applications

In this section, we work through a pair of examples that go beyond the two-site chain—first the three-site chain, and then (going beyond tree level) the box and kite graphs. We then discuss the connection between our coaction and the kinematic flow of the wavefunction of the universe, and (separately) the monodromy group that governs these periods. Finally, we highlight simplifications that occur in the coaction when considering in-in correlators, rather than the wavefunction of the universe.

Whenever possible, we have compared our coaction formula with the coaction of hypergeometric functions in
26
;
2
, and found perfect agreement. Additionally, we have checked the compatibility of our coaction with the coaction on MPLs by comparing the
\alpha_{\bullet}\sim 0
expansion of the coaction against the coaction of the polylogarithmic coefficients of the
\alpha_{\bullet}\sim 0
expansion, to several orders in
\alpha_{\bullet}
.

5.1
The three-site chain graph

As detailed in section
4.6
, the space of acyclic minors label graphical zonotopes, each of which represents a disconnected group of periods.
Because of this, the period matrix is block diagonal; in the case of the three-site chain,

\displaystyle\underline{\bm{P}}=\underline{\bm{P}}[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_bb.pdf}}]\oplus\underline{\bm{P}}[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_sb.pdf}}]\oplus\underline{\bm{P}}[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_bs.pdf}}]\oplus\underline{\bm{P}}[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf}}]

(5.1)

As can be checked, the periods associated to
\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_bb.pdf}}
,
\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_sb.pdf}}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_bs.pdf}}
evaluate to two-site periods with shifted kinematics, up to multiplication by a power function:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_bb.pdf}}:

\displaystyle\quad\begin{matrix}{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}=\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/3chain/3chain_bb.pdf}\end{matrix}\quad

\displaystyle\underline{\bm{P}}[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_bb.pdf}}]

\displaystyle:=\begin{pmatrix}f^{\alpha_{1}}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_bb_R1.pdf}}f^{\alpha_{2}}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_bb_R2.pdf}}f^{\alpha_{3}}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_bb_R3.pdf}}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(5.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_sb.pdf}}:

\displaystyle\quad\begin{matrix}{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}=\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/3chain/3chain_sb.pdf}\\
{\mathfrak{g}}_{3}=\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/3chain/3chain_rb.pdf}\\
{\mathfrak{g}}_{4}=\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/3chain/3chain_lb.pdf}\end{matrix}

\displaystyle\underline{\bm{P}}[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_sb.pdf}}]

\displaystyle:=\underline{\bm{P}}[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}]\;f^{\alpha_{3}}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_sb_R2.pdf}}\,,

(5.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_bs.pdf}}:

\displaystyle\quad\begin{matrix}{\mathfrak{g}}_{5}=\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/3chain/3chain_bs.pdf}\\
{\mathfrak{g}}_{6}=\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/3chain/3chain_br.pdf}\\
{\mathfrak{g}}_{7}=\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/3chain/3chain_bl.pdf}\end{matrix}

\displaystyle\underline{\bm{P}}[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_bs.pdf}}]

\displaystyle:=f^{\alpha_{1}}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_bs_R1.pdf}}\;\left(\underline{\bm{P}}[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}]|_{\alpha_{i}\to\alpha_{i+1},X_{i}\to X_{i+1},Y_{12}\to Y_{23}}\right)\,,

(5.4)

where we recall that each of the letters
f_{\mathsf{r}}
is a polynomial, as given in (
2.49
). Thus, the new periods we encounter are associated only with
\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf}}
:

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{g}}_{8}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf}\,,

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{g}}_{9}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/3chain/3chain_ls.pdf}\,,

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{g}}_{10}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/3chain/3chain_rs.pdf}\,,

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{g}}_{11}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/3chain/3chain_sl.pdf}\,,

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{g}}_{12}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/3chain/3chain_sr.pdf}\,,

(5.5)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{g}}_{13}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/3chain/3chain_ll.pdf}\,,

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{g}}_{14}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/3chain/3chain_lr.pdf}\,,

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{g}}_{15}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/3chain/3chain_rl.pdf}\,,

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{g}}_{16}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/3chain/3chain_rr.pdf}\,.

As always, the diagonal of the period matrix are power functions

\displaystyle\mathrm{diag}\underline{\bm{P}}[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf}}]:=\bigg(\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha_{1}\right)\Gamma\left(\alpha_{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\alpha_{3}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3}\right)}f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss_R1.pdf}}^{\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3}},\,\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{2})\Gamma(\alpha_{3})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3})}f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ls_R1.pdf}}^{\alpha_{1}}f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ls_R2.pdf}}^{\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3}},\,

\displaystyle\qquad\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{2})\Gamma(\alpha_{3})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3})}f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rs_R1.pdf}}^{\alpha_{1}}f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rs_R2.pdf}}^{\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3}},\,\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{1})\Gamma(\alpha_{2})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2})}f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_sl_R1.pdf}}^{\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2}}f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_sl_R2.pdf}}^{\alpha_{3}},\,\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{1})\Gamma(\alpha_{2})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2})}f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_sr_R1.pdf}}^{\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2}}f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_sr_R2.pdf}}^{\alpha_{3}},\,

(5.6)

\displaystyle\qquad f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ll_R1.pdf}}^{\alpha_{1}}f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ll_R2.pdf}}^{\alpha_{2}}f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ll_R3.pdf}}^{\alpha_{3}},\,2f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_lr_R1.pdf}}^{\alpha_{1}}f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_lr_R2.pdf}}^{\alpha_{2}}f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_lr_R3.pdf}}^{\alpha_{3}},\,f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rl_R1.pdf}}^{\alpha_{1}}f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rl_R2.pdf}}^{\alpha_{2}}f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rl_R3.pdf}}^{\alpha_{3}},\,f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rr_R1.pdf}}^{\alpha_{1}}f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rr_R2.pdf}}^{\alpha_{2}}f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rr_R3.pdf}}^{\alpha_{3}}\bigg)\,.

The most interesting periods are the
P_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf},{\mathfrak{h}}}
-entries.
There are four
P_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf},{\mathfrak{h}}}
that are analogous to the off-diagonals of the two-site chain:

\displaystyle\frac{P_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf},\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ls.pdf}}}{P_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf},\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf}}}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{1}(\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3})\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3}{+}2)}\;{}_{2}\tilde{F}_{1}\left(1,\alpha_{1}{+}1;\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3}{+}2;\frac{f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss_R1.pdf}}}{f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ls_R1.pdf}}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle=-\alpha_{1}(\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3})\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3}{+}2)}\;{}_{2}\tilde{F}_{1}\left(1,\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3}{+}1;\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3}{+}2;\frac{f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss_R1.pdf}}}{f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ls_R2.pdf}}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle P_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf},\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rs.pdf}}

\displaystyle=-P_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf},\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ls.pdf}}|_{Y_{12}\to-Y_{12}}\,,

\displaystyle P_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf},\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_sl.pdf}}

\displaystyle=-P_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf},\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ls.pdf}}|_{\alpha_{1}\leftrightarrow\alpha_{3},X_{1}\leftrightarrow X_{3},Y_{12}\to-Y_{23}}\,,

\displaystyle P_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf},\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_sr.pdf}}

\displaystyle=P_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf},\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ls.pdf}}|_{\alpha_{1}\leftrightarrow\alpha_{3},X_{1}\leftrightarrow X_{3},Y_{12}\to Y_{23}}\,.

(5.7)

The most complex periods in this row are Appell
F_{3}
’s (a specific example of Lauricella’s
F_{B}
functions).
These are the hypergeometric functions that go beyond those we saw in the two-site chain:
16
16

16

Because
\varphi_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_lr.pdf}}
is a sum of two
\mathrm{dlog}
-forms (recall (
4.46
)), there is also the two term representation:

\displaystyle\frac{P_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf},\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_lr.pdf}}}{P_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf},\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf}}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\Gamma(\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3})}{\Gamma(3+\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3})}\bigg[\tilde{F}_{B}\left(1,1;\alpha_{1}{+}1,\alpha_{2}{+}1;\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3}{+}3;\frac{f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss_R1.pdf}}}{f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ls_R1.pdf}}},\frac{f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss_R1.pdf}}}{f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_lr_R2.pdf}}}\right)

\displaystyle+\tilde{F}_{B}\left(1,1;\alpha_{2}{+}1,\alpha_{3}{+}1;\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3}{+}3;\frac{f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss_R1.pdf}}}{f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_lr_R2.pdf}}},\frac{f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss_R1.pdf}}}{f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_sr_R2.pdf}}}\right)\bigg]\,.

\displaystyle\frac{P_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf},\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ll.pdf}}}{P_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf},\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\Gamma(\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3})}{\Gamma(3+\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3})}\tilde{F}_{B}\left(1,1;\alpha_{1}{+}1,\alpha_{3}{+}1;\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3}{+}3;\frac{f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss_R1.pdf}}}{f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ls_R1.pdf}}},\frac{f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss_R1.pdf}}}{f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_sl_R2.pdf}}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle P_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf},\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_lr.pdf}}

\displaystyle=P_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf},\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ll.pdf}}|_{Y_{23}\to-Y_{23}}\,,

\displaystyle P_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf},\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rl.pdf}}

\displaystyle=P_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf},\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ll.pdf}}|_{Y_{12}\to-Y_{12}}\,,

\displaystyle P_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf},\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_rr.pdf}}

\displaystyle=P_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ss.pdf},\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ll.pdf}}|_{Y_{12}\to-Y_{12},Y_{23}\to-Y_{23}}

(5.8)

where

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{B}(\bm{a};\bm{b};c;\bm{z})=F_{B}(\bm{a};\bm{b};c;\bm{z})\;\prod_{k=1}^{m}z_{k}

(5.9)

is a rescaled Lauricella
F_{B}
function.

The rest of the entries in this block are simpler. For the rows
P_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ll.pdf},{\mathfrak{h}}}

, the only nonzero terms are the diagonal entries, which are given in (

).
In the remaining four rows, corresponding to

, the only nonzero off-diagonal entries are

\displaystyle P_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ls.pdf},\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ll.pdf}}

\displaystyle=f^{\alpha_{1}}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ls_R1.pdf}}\;\left(P_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs},\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gl}}|_{\alpha_{i}\to\alpha_{i+1},X_{1}\to X_{2}+Y_{12},X_{2}\to X_{3},Y_{12}\to Y_{23}}\right)\,,

(5.10)

\displaystyle P_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_ls.pdf},\includegraphics[scale]{figs/3chain/3chain_lr.pdf}}

\displaystyle\Delta\left[\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/P_ss_ll.pdf}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}}{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}}\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/P_ss_ss.pdf}\otimes\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/P_ss_ll.pdf}+\frac{\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}}{\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}}\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/P_ss_ls.pdf}\otimes\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/P_ls_ll.pdf}

\displaystyle+\frac{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/P_ss_sl.pdf}\otimes\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/P_sl_ll.pdf}+\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/P_ss_ll.pdf}\otimes\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/P_ll_ll.pdf}\,.

(5.11)

F_{3}

.

For periods whose contour corresponds to a degenerate cut tubing, we write out the linear combination of periods dictated by the linear combination of cut contours (

\displaystyle P_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/g_lr.pdf},\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/g_lr.pdf}}=\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/P_lr_lr1.pdf}-\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/P_lr_lr2.pdf}\,.

The coaction works as before; the only thing that changes is that the factor of

This is how degenerate contours are represented both in the

5.2
Beyond tree level

The one-loop four-gon graph has a basis of 226 elements, making it too large to showcase in full detail here.
Instead, we focus on a particular integral in the most complicated sector, which corresponds to the zonotope
\smash{\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/boxZono_ssss.pdf}}}
.
Even this zonotope contains 51 acyclic minors (14 dimension-0 facets, 24 dimension-1 facets, 12 dimension-2 facets, and 1 dimension-3 facet).
Furthermore, we focus on the coaction of
, which only mixes with 16 periods from this sector.
Explicitly,

\displaystyle\Delta\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/C1234Box.pdf}\;

\displaystyle{=}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/C1234Box.pdf}\;{\otimes}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/CBox.pdf}\;{+}\frac{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}{\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2}}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/C1234Box1.pdf}\;{\otimes}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/C1Box.pdf}\;

\displaystyle{+}\frac{\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}}{\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3}}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/C1234Box2.pdf}\;{\otimes}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/C2Box.pdf}\;{+}\frac{\alpha_{3}\alpha_{4}}{\alpha_{3}{+}\alpha_{4}}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/C1234Box3.pdf}\;{\otimes}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/C3Box.pdf}\;

(5.13)

\displaystyle{+}\frac{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}}{\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3}}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/C1234Box12.pdf}\;{\otimes}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/C12Box.pdf}\;{+}\frac{\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\alpha_{4}}{\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3}{+}\alpha_{4}}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/C1234Box23.pdf}\;{\otimes}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/C23Box.pdf}\;
