where for the
\alpha_{i}
labels we adopt the convention that the vertices are ordered clockwise, starting from the top left.
Note that for loop graphs, the acyclic condition starts to matter. In this example,
the decorated minors

\displaystyle\left\{\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/box4.pdf}\;,\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/box34.pdf}\;,\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/box41.pdf}\;,\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/box24.pdf}\;,\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/box123.pdf}\;,\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/box234.pdf}\;,\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/box341.pdf}\;,\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/box412.pdf}\;\right\}

(5.14)

do not appear because they contain a cycle of directed edges, once the pinched edges are contracted.

As a two-loop example, let us now consider the kite graph.
The physical subspace for this graph is spanned by 614 acyclic minors, which are organized into 32 zonotopes. The largest zonotope,
\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/ZkiteMaxPinch.pdf}}
, contains 59 periods (one for each facet: 18 dimension-0 facets, 28 dimension-1 facets, 8 dimension-2 facets, 4 dimension-3 facets and 1 dimension-4 facet).
Like for the box, we will only exhibit the coaction for one of the maximally-complex periods that appears:

\displaystyle\Delta\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/P81.pdf}\;{=}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/P81.pdf}\;{\otimes}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/P11.pdf}\;{+}\frac{\alpha_{4}\alpha_{1}}{\alpha_{4}{+}\alpha_{1}}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/P82.pdf}\;{\otimes}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/P21.pdf}\;

\displaystyle{+}\frac{\alpha_{3}\alpha_{4}}{\alpha_{3}{+}\alpha_{4}}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/P83.pdf}\;{\otimes}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/P31.pdf}\;{+}\frac{\alpha_{3}\alpha_{4}\alpha_{1}}{\alpha_{3}{+}\alpha_{4}{+}\alpha_{1}}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/P84.pdf}\;{\otimes}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/P41.pdf}\;

(5.15)

\displaystyle{+}\frac{\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}}{\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3}}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/P85.pdf}\;{\otimes}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/P51.pdf}\;{+}\frac{\alpha_{4}\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}}{(\alpha_{4}{+}\alpha_{1})(\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3})}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/P86.pdf}\;{\otimes}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/P61.pdf}\;

\displaystyle{+}\frac{\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\alpha_{4}}{\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3}{+}\alpha_{4}}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/P87.pdf}\;{\otimes}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/P71.pdf}\;{+}\frac{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\alpha_{4}}{\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3}{+}\alpha_{4}}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/P88.pdf}\;{\otimes}\;\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/P81.pdf}\;\,,

where we have again labeled the vertices clockwise, this time starting from the top.

Although the 4-gon has
226
basis elements in total, the integral we have chosen above only mixes with
16
of them; this is representative of the more general behavior that, even at one loop, the block-diagonal structure of the period matrix dictated by zonotopes (see section
4.6
) keeps the coaction of any particular period manageable.

5.3
Kinematic flow from the coaction

The coaction fully encodes the system of differential equations that govern the space of periods that we associate with each FRW graph. In particular, in the graphical language of acyclic minors, we have the following differential equation:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}[\gamma|\varphi_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\rangle=\sum_{\mathfrak{h}\in\mathrm{pinch}({\mathfrak{g}})}\frac{[\gamma|\varphi_{\mathfrak{h}}\rangle}{\langle\check{\varphi}_{\mathfrak{h}}|\varphi_{\mathfrak{h}}\rangle}\;\mathrm{d}W_{1}^{\mathrm{MPL,\alpha}}\Big[[\gamma_{\mathfrak{h}}|\varphi_{\mathfrak{g}}\rangle\Big]\implies A_{{\mathfrak{g}}\mathfrak{h}}=\frac{\mathrm{d}W_{1}^{\mathrm{MPL,\alpha}}\Big[[\gamma_{\mathfrak{h}}|\varphi_{\mathfrak{g}}\rangle\Big]}{\langle\check{\varphi}_{\mathfrak{h}}|\varphi_{\mathfrak{h}}\rangle}\,.

(5.16)

In order to compute these differential equations, we see that we must compute the weight-one part of each of the periods
[\gamma_{\mathfrak{h}}|\varphi_{\mathfrak{g}}\rangle
in our basis; an explicit formula for this weight-one part is given in (
A.15
) of appendix
A
. There, we see that there are two situations in which the period
[\gamma_{\mathfrak{h}}|\varphi_{\mathfrak{g}}\rangle
can have a non-trivial weight-one part.
The first is when
{\mathfrak{h}}={\mathfrak{g}}
; then, we have

\displaystyle W_{1}^{\mathrm{MPL,\alpha}}\Big[[\gamma_{\mathfrak{g}}|\varphi_{\mathfrak{g}}\rangle\Big]=\langle\check{\varphi}_{\mathfrak{g}}|\varphi_{\mathfrak{g}}\rangle\left(\sum_{\mathsf{r}\in\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\beta_{\mathsf{r}}\ \log f_{\mathsf{r}}\right)\,,\qquad{\mathfrak{h}}={\mathfrak{g}}\,.

(5.17)

The second case is when a region
\mathsf{r}^{*}
of
{\mathfrak{h}}
splits into two regions of
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
That is,
\mathsf{r}^{*}\in\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{h}}
such that
\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{h}}\setminus(\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{h}})=\{\mathsf{r}^{*}\}
and
\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{g}}\setminus(\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{h}}\cap\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{g}})=\{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\uparrow},\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\downarrow}\}
.
17
17

17

Recall that, by definition, the regions
\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\uparrow}
and
\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\downarrow}
are both connected subgraphs with pinched edges.

Pictorially, we represent this as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/gsg.pdf}\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{and}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/grg.pdf}\,,

(5.18)

\displaystyle\mathrm{or}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/gsg.pdf}\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{and}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/glg.pdf}\,.

Here, the gray dashed lines represent possible connections to other regions via broken edges.
Then, the weight-one part only depends on how
\mathsf{r}^{*}
breaks into
\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\uparrow}
and
\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\downarrow}
:

\displaystyle W_{1}^{\mathrm{MPL,\alpha}}\Big[[\gamma_{\mathfrak{h}}|\varphi_{\mathfrak{g}}\rangle\Big]

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}C_{{\mathfrak{g}}{\mathfrak{g}}}\log\frac{f_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/f_u_right_dc.pdf}}}{f_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/f_uc_right_d.pdf}}}&\mathfrak{h}=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/gsg.pdf}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad{\mathfrak{g}}=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/grg.pdf}\,,\\[10.00002pt]
C_{{\mathfrak{g}}{\mathfrak{g}}}\log\frac{f_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/f_uc_left_d.pdf}}}{f_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/f_u_left_dc.pdf}}}&\mathfrak{h}=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/gsg.pdf}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad{\mathfrak{g}}=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/glg.pdf}\end{cases}

(5.19)

(see appendix
A
for details). Putting (
5.16
), (
5.17
) and (
5.19
) together, we reproduce kinematic flow, as derived in
57
:

\displaystyle A_{{\mathfrak{g}}\mathfrak{h}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\sum_{\mathsf{r}\in\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\beta_{\mathsf{r}}\ \mathrm{dlog}f_{\mathsf{r}}&{\mathfrak{h}}={\mathfrak{g}}\,,\\[10.00002pt]
\frac{C_{{\mathfrak{g}}{\mathfrak{g}}}}{C_{{\mathfrak{h}}{\mathfrak{h}}}}\mathrm{dlog}\frac{f_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/f_u_right_dc.pdf}}}{f_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/f_uc_right_d.pdf}}}&\mathfrak{h}=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/gsg.pdf}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad{\mathfrak{g}}=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/grg.pdf}\,,\\[10.00002pt]
\frac{C_{{\mathfrak{g}}{\mathfrak{g}}}}{C_{{\mathfrak{h}}{\mathfrak{h}}}}\mathrm{dlog}\frac{f_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/f_uc_left_d.pdf}}}{f_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/f_u_left_dc.pdf}}}&\mathfrak{h}=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/gsg.pdf}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad{\mathfrak{g}}=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/glg.pdf}\,.\end{cases}

(5.20)

5.4
Monodromy from the coaction

Recall from section
3
that, just as derivatives only act on the last entry of the coaction, discontinuities only act on the first entry. Thus, given the coaction

\displaystyle\Delta[\gamma_{\mathfrak{g}}|\varphi\rangle=\sum_{\mathfrak{h}:{\mathfrak{g}}\in\mathrm{pinch}({\mathfrak{h}})}\frac{[\gamma_{\mathfrak{g}}|\varphi_{\mathfrak{h}}\rangle}{\langle\check{\varphi}_{\mathfrak{h}}|\varphi_{\mathfrak{h}}\rangle}\otimes[\gamma_{\mathfrak{h}}|\varphi\rangle\,,

(5.21)

we have that

\displaystyle\text{Disc}_{\bullet}[\gamma_{\mathfrak{g}}|\varphi\rangle=\sum_{\mathfrak{h}:{\mathfrak{g}}\in\mathrm{pinch}({\mathfrak{h}})}\frac{\text{Disc}_{\bullet}W_{1}^{\mathrm{MPL,\alpha}}\Big[[\gamma_{\mathfrak{g}}|\varphi_{\mathfrak{h}}\rangle\Big]}{\langle\check{\varphi}_{\mathfrak{h}}|\varphi_{\mathfrak{h}}\rangle}[\gamma_{\mathfrak{h}}|\varphi\rangle:=\sum_{{\mathfrak{h}}}M_{{\mathfrak{g}}{\mathfrak{h}}}[\gamma_{\mathfrak{h}}|\varphi\rangle\,,

(5.22)

where the last equality defines the
monodromy matrix

M_{{\mathfrak{g}}{\mathfrak{h}}}
,
and
\text{Disc}_{\bullet}
denotes a discontinuity with respect to a given kinematic locus.
In appendix
A
, we show that the entries of this matrix are only nonzero when one of the graphical conditions (on
{\mathfrak{g}}
and
{\mathfrak{h}}
) are satisfied:

\displaystyle M_{{\mathfrak{g}}{\mathfrak{h}}}=\begin{cases}\sum_{\mathsf{r}\in\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\beta_{\mathsf{r}}\ \text{Disc}_{\bullet}\left[\log f_{\mathsf{r}}\right]&{\mathfrak{h}}={\mathfrak{g}}\,,\\[10.00002pt]
\text{Disc}_{\bullet}\left[\log\frac{f_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/f_u_right_dc.pdf}}}{f_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/f_uc_right_d.pdf}}}\right]&\mathfrak{g}=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/gsg.pdf}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad{\mathfrak{h}}=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/grg.pdf}\,,\\[10.00002pt]
\text{Disc}_{\bullet}\left[\log\frac{f_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/f_uc_left_d.pdf}}}{f_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/f_u_left_dc.pdf}}}\right]&\mathfrak{g}=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/gsg.pdf}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad{\mathfrak{h}}=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/glg.pdf}\,.\end{cases}

(5.23)

Note that the discontinuity operator will only return a nonzero result if we compute a discontinuity with respect to one of the branch cuts that starts where the arguments of these logarithms either vanish or become infinite.

At any order in the
\alpha_{\bullet}
-expansion, the monodromy matrix
M_{{\mathfrak{g}}{\mathfrak{h}}}
tells us how to build the symbol starting from the first entry (just as the connection matrix
A_{{\mathfrak{g}}{\mathfrak{h}}}
instructs us how to build the symbol starting from the last entry). As an example, consider the two-site chain graph.
It has the monodromy matrix

\displaystyle\underline{\bm{M}}[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}]{=}\begin{pmatrix}(\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2})\text{Disc}_{\bullet}[\log f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_Rs1}}]&\text{Disc}_{\bullet}\left[\log\frac{f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_Rr2}}}{f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_Rr1}}}\right]&\text{Disc}_{\bullet}\left[\log\frac{f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_Rl1}}}{f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_Rl2}}}\right]\\
&\Large\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha_{1}\text{Disc}_{\bullet}[\log f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_Rr2}}]\\
\hskip 16.38895pt{+}\alpha_{2}\text{Disc}_{\bullet}[\log f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_Rr1}}]\end{subarray}&\\
&&\Large\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha_{1}\text{Disc}_{\bullet}[\log f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_Rl2}}]\\
\hskip 16.38895pt{+}\alpha_{2}\text{Disc}_{\bullet}[\log f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_Rl1}}]\end{subarray}\end{pmatrix}

(5.24)

In fact, we can make the discontinuity structure of the
\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}
sector more transparent by writing out the monodromy matrix associated with each logarithmic branch point
f_{\mathsf{r}}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\underline{\bm{M}}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_Rs1}}[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}]}{2\pi i}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2}&0&0\\
&0&\\
&&0\end{pmatrix}\,,

(5.25)

\displaystyle\frac{\underline{\bm{M}}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_Rr1}}[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}]}{2\pi i}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1&0\\
&\alpha_{2}&\\
&&0\end{pmatrix}\,,

\displaystyle\frac{\underline{\bm{M}}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_Rr2}}[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}]}{2\pi i}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\
&\alpha_{1}&\\
&&0\end{pmatrix}\,,

\displaystyle\frac{\underline{\bm{M}}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_Rl1}}[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}]}{2\pi i}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\
&\alpha_{2}&\\
&&0\end{pmatrix}\,,

\displaystyle\frac{\underline{\bm{M}}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_Rl2}}[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}]}{2\pi i}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1&0\\
&\alpha_{1}&\\
&&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

These monodromy matrices predict the non-trivial sequential discontinuities that can appear in the FRW periods contained in
\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}
.
For example,

\displaystyle\frac{\text{Disc}_{f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_Rs1}}=0}}{2\pi i}\begin{pmatrix}[\gamma_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}|\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}\rangle\\
[\gamma_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}|\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}\rangle\\
\cancel{[\gamma_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gl}}|\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}\rangle}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2}&0&0\\
&0&\\
&&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}[\gamma_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}|\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}\rangle\\
[\gamma_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}|\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}\rangle\\
0\end{pmatrix}\,,

(5.26)

\displaystyle\frac{\text{Disc}_{f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_Rr1}}=0}}{2\pi i}\begin{pmatrix}[\gamma_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}|\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}\rangle\\
[\gamma_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}|\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}\rangle\\
\cancel{[\gamma_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gl}}|\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}\rangle}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1&0\\
&\alpha_{1}&\\
&&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}[\gamma_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}|\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}\rangle\\
[\gamma_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}|\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}\rangle\\
0\end{pmatrix}\,,

(5.27)

\displaystyle\frac{\text{Disc}_{f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_Rr2}}=0}}{2\pi i}\begin{pmatrix}[\gamma_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}|\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}\rangle\\
[\gamma_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}|\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}\rangle\\
\cancel{[\gamma_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gl}}|\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}\rangle}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\
&\alpha_{2}&\\
&&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}[\gamma_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}|\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}\rangle\\
[\gamma_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}|\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}\rangle\\
0\end{pmatrix}\,,

(5.28)

where we have drawn a slash through periods that identically vanish. All discontinuities with repspect to other branch points also vanish.
Since the FRW integrals we are considering do not give rise to algebraic discontinuities, each nonzero sequence of discontinuities yields a distinct word in the symbol.
18
18

18

For similar examples of this monodromy matrix construction in the context of flat-space Feynman integrals, see for instance
19
.

The monodromy matrix of FRW integrals can also be seen to encode nontrivial types of analytic structure. For instance, the sequences of nonzero discontinuities that appear in the integral
{[\gamma_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}|\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}\rangle}
can be diagrammatically summarized as follows:

{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{161.54102pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{

(5.29)

where each arrow represents a nonzero entry in the matrix (
5.24
). Namely, the symbol of the
\alpha_{\bullet}\sim 0
expansion of
{[\gamma_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}|\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}\rangle}
can be read off of this diagram by considering all directed paths that begin at the
{[\gamma_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}|\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}\rangle}
node; each path represents a sequence of nonzero discontinuities, corresponding to the sequence of arrows one has followed. Even though this integral has an especially simple structure, we can still read nontrivial information off of this diagram. In particular, we see that by computing discontinuities with respect to
f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_Rr1}}
or
f_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_Rr2}}
we can move from the
{[\gamma_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gs}}|\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}\rangle}
to the
[\gamma_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}|\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figs/2chain_gr}}\rangle
node in the diagram; conversely, there are no discontinuities that take us back in the other direction. This phenomenon—that some discontinuities no longer exist once others have been computed—also appears in flat-space Feynman integrals, where it goes under the name of the
hierarchical principle

21
;
74
;
79
;
68
;
66
. In this case, the implications of the hierarchical principle are not drastic, since the same pair of discontinuities that take us between nodes in this diagram also appear via arrows that send each node back to itself; however, in more complicated examples, these hierarchical constraints hold important implications for the structure of the symbol.

5.5
In-in correlators

Let us finish this section by considering interesting simplifications that occur for in-in correlators. Following
13
, we have that the only difference between wavefunction coefficients and in-in correlators (in the class of theories we are considering) is the removal of the alternating sign in equation (
4.43
), namely

\displaystyle\psi_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle=\int u_{\mathcal{G}}\;\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}\,,

\displaystyle\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{E}\subset\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{G}}}(-1)^{|\mathcal{E}|}\sum_{{\mathfrak{g}}\in\mathcal{A}^{\varnothing}_{\mathcal{E}}(\mathcal{G})}\varphi_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\,,

(5.30)

\displaystyle\downarrow

\displaystyle\downarrow

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle_{\mathrm{in-in}}

\displaystyle=\int u_{\mathcal{G}}\;\varphi_{\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle}\,,

\displaystyle\varphi_{\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathcal{E}\subset\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{G}}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{g}}\in\mathcal{A}^{\varnothing}_{\mathcal{E}}(\mathcal{G})}\varphi_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\,.

At tree-level, the two formulas are of the same relative complexity.
However, at loop-level, cancellations occur such that only the fully-oriented and fully-disconnected acyclic minors contribute:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle}

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\mathfrak{g}}\in\mathrm{Vert}(\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{G}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chain_gs.pdf}}})}\varphi_{{\mathfrak{g}}}+\sum_{{\mathfrak{g}}\in\mathrm{Vert}(\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{G}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chain_gb.pdf}}})}\varphi_{{\mathfrak{g}}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{if}\;\mathrm{loops}(\mathcal{G})>0\,,

(5.31)

where
\mathcal{G}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chain_gs.pdf}}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chain_gb.pdf}}
denote the zonotope where all edges of
\mathcal{G}
are either replaced by pinched edges
or broken edges
, and
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{G}_{\bullet}})
denotes the acyclic minors that correspond to the vertices of the zonotope.
For
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{G}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chain_gb.pdf}}}
, there is only a single vertex labeled by
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathcal{G}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chain_gb.pdf}}
.
On the other hand, the vertices of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{G}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/2chain_gs.pdf}}}
correspond to the acyclic minors of
\mathcal{G}
that involve only oriented edges.

Figure 7
:
The relevant zonotopes for the in-in correlator for the one-loop two-gon:

\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/bub_pp.pdf}}
(right) and

\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/bub_bb.pdf}}
(left).

Recently, it was observed that the cancellations at the integrand level (
5.31
), also lead to simplifications after loop integration
41
; namely, there is an effective weight drop when passing from the de Sitter wavefunction coefficient to the in-in correlator.
19
19

19

Technically, both the wavefunction coefficient and the in-in correlator have the same weight.
However, in the case of the correlator, part of this weight is accounted for by trivial factors of
\pi
.
Therefore, the functions appearing in the correlator are genuinely simpler.

While we have not given any attention to the integrals over spatial loop momentum in this work, our coaction applies straightforwardly to the loop integrand, since the function space of in-in correlator (loop) integrands is a subspace of the function space of wavefunction coefficient (loop) integrands.

For example, consider the loop-level integrand of the one-loop bubble in-in correlator:

\displaystyle\left\langle\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/bub.pdf}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}u_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/bub.pdf}}\;\varphi_{\left\langle\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/bub.pdf}\right\rangle}\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\varphi_{\left\langle\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/bub.pdf}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/bub_ll.pdf}}+\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/bub_rr.pdf}}+\varphi_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/bub_bb.pdf}}\,,

(5.32)

where we have summed over the vertices of the zonotopes depicted in figure
7
.
In terms of our basis integrals, its coaction is simply

\displaystyle\Delta\left\langle\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/bub.pdf}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}\;\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/bub_pp.pdf}\;\otimes\left(\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/C121_Bub_ll.pdf}+\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/C121_Bub_rr.pdf}\right)

(5.33)

\displaystyle+\;\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/bub_ll.pdf}\;\otimes\;\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/C121_2_Bub_ll.pdf}\;+\;\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/bub_rr.pdf}\;\otimes\;\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/C121_1_Bub_rr.pdf}\;+\;\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/bub_bb.pdf}\;\otimes\;\includegraphics[scale,align]{figs/C1_2_Bub_bb.pdf}\;.

Thus, we see that all the same technology carries over.

Although we will not explore the analytic structure of in-in correlators further in this work, is interesting to note that most terms in the discontinuity of such correlators are proportional to integrals that involve contours that enforce total energy conservation.
This means that for most terms in the discontinuity, we end up integrating over the (integrand of the) flat-space amplitude
\text{Res}_{B_{\mathrm{tot}}=0}[u_{\mathcal{G}}\;\varphi_{\mathcal{G}}]=(u_{\mathcal{G}}|_{B_{\mathrm{tot}}=0})\;\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathbf{X}+\mathbf{x},\mathbf{Y})|_{B_{\mathrm{tot}}=0}\;\mathrm{d}^{|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{G}}|-1}\mathbf{x}
.

6
Conclusion

In this work, we formulated a graphical coaction for the wavefunction of the universe, which applies to all FRW integrals that contribute to theories of conformally-coupled scalars in power-law FRW cosmologies.
By leveraging the twisted (co)homology of the associated integral families, the coaction decomposes wavefunction coefficients in these theories into tensor products of simpler FRW periods, each of which can be represented by a decoration of the original Feynman diagram.
This yields a unified, graphical language that encapsulates both the differential equations and the sequential discontinuities of cosmological correlators.
Our construction is analogous to (and may provide hints for studying) the diagrammatic coaction for flat-space Feynman integrals, but involves a richer graphical language that incorporates the direction of time.

As part of our analysis, we demonstrated that the coaction formula considered in
2
;
26
;
22
straightforwardly generalizes to the setting in which not all divisors are twisted (which is to say, to partial/relative twisted (co)homology).
This is expected, because hypergeometric functions whose arguments take integer values are generally smooth, as has been seen in the context of flat-space Feynman integrals
4
;
5
;
3
.
However, even though hypergeometric functions themselves often have smooth limits, the relevant (co)homology changes discontinuously.
Thus, our analysis in this paper has been restricted to the (co)homology that actually appears for FRW integrals, rather than more general (co)homologies that reduce to the FRW (co)homology in special limits.

We have also developed a user-friendly web application that computes the graphical coaction of any graph:

​.
A
Mathematica
notebook with the same functionality is also hosted on the repository:

.

Several natural extensions of our investigation suggest themselves.
It would be interesting to connect this construction more explicitly to the diagrammatic coaction for flat-space Feynman integrals
5
;
7
;
3
in the
\varepsilon\to-1
limit, and to explore whether the coaction structure persists for theories with spinning fields, non-power-law scale factors, or non-conformally coupled scalars.
It would also be interesting to see how this can be applied to the recent understanding of in-in correlators as dispersive integrals
40
.
In particular, most of the structure of in-in correlators at loop-level should be determined by flat-space-like physics, as discussed at the end of section
5.5
.
Lastly, it would be worthwhile to connect our formalism to that of the cosmological cutting rules and cosmological optical theorem
76
;
64
. We leave these investigations to future work.

Appendix A
Weight-one part of the basis integrals

Diagonal pairings
[{\mathfrak{g}}|{\mathfrak{g}}\rangle
are proportional to (products of) power functions:
[{\mathfrak{g}}|{\mathfrak{g}}\rangle=c_{\mathfrak{g}}(\alpha_{\bullet})\prod_{\mathsf{r}\in\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{g}}}f_{\mathsf{r}}^{\beta_{\mathsf{r}}}
where
\beta_{\mathsf{r}}=\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{r}}}\alpha_{v}
are linear in the
\alpha_{\bullet}
.
The small
\alpha_{i}
(
\beta_{\mathsf{r}}
) expansion of a power function is

\displaystyle f_{\mathsf{r}}^{\beta_{\mathsf{r}}}=1+\beta_{\mathsf{r}}\log f_{\mathsf{r}}+\mathcal{O}(\beta_{\mathsf{r}})\,.

(A.1)

We also know that the leading term of

[\gamma_{\mathfrak{h}}|\varphi_{\mathfrak{g}}\rangle

in the small
\alpha_{i}
limit is the self-intersection number

\langle\check{\varphi}_{\mathfrak{g}}|\varphi_{\mathfrak{g}}\rangle
:

\displaystyle[\gamma_{\mathfrak{g}}|\varphi_{\mathfrak{g}}\rangle=\langle\check{\varphi}_{\mathfrak{g}}|\varphi_{\mathfrak{g}}\rangle\Big(1+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{\bullet})\Big)\,,

(A.2)

and hence

\displaystyle W_{1}^{\mathrm{MPL,\alpha}}\Big[[\gamma_{\mathfrak{g}}|\varphi_{\mathfrak{g}}\rangle\Big]

\displaystyle=\langle\check{\varphi}_{\mathfrak{g}}|\varphi_{\mathfrak{g}}\rangle\left(\sum_{\mathsf{r}\in\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\beta_{\mathsf{r}}\ \log f_{\mathsf{r}}\right)\,.

(A.3)

These statements can be proved by repeated use of the basic identity

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\theta(-x)\theta(x+c)x^{\alpha}(-x-c)^{\beta}\mathrm{dlog}\frac{x+c}{x}=\frac{\theta(c)(-c)^{\alpha+\beta}\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(\beta)}{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)}\,,

(A.4)

whenever a diagonal period is not fully localized by the contour.

The off-diagonal pairings
[{\mathfrak{g}}|\mathfrak{h}\rangle
are genuine hypergeometric functions.
They contain a weight-one MPL term if
and only if a region
\mathsf{r}^{*}
of
{\mathfrak{h}}
splits to make
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
That is,
\mathsf{r}^{*}=\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{h}}\setminus(\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{h}})
and
\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{g}}\setminus(\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{h}}\cap\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{g}})=\{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\uparrow},\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\downarrow}\}
.

20

If
{\mathfrak{h}}
is a
p
-pinch of
{\mathfrak{g}}
, we can perform
|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{G}}|-p
of the integrations via (
A.4
) in
[{\mathfrak{g}}|\mathfrak{h}\rangle
where each results in a power function.
The remaining
p
-integrations have
p
-many poles outside of the twisted loci.
To get the integral as a series in
\alpha_{\bullet}
, we expand the twist inside the integral and note that, after integration, the lowest weight MPL has weight
p
.

Pictorially, we represent this as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/gsg.pdf}\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{and}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/grg.pdf}\,,

(A.5)

\displaystyle\mathrm{or}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/gsg.pdf}\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{and}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/glg.pdf}\,.

Here, the gray dashed lines represent possible connections to other regions via broken or oriented edges.
Since all other regions are the same, we can focus solely on how
\mathsf{r}^{*}
is broken into
\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\uparrow}
and
\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\downarrow}
.
Next, note that up to an overall sign, that we will reinstate later,

\displaystyle{[}\gamma_{{\mathfrak{h}}}|\varphi_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\rangle

\displaystyle\propto|{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}|\left(\prod_{\mathsf{r}\in\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{h}}\setminus\mathsf{r}^{*}}\int_{\Delta_{\mathsf{r}}}\left(\prod_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{r}}}x_{v}^{\alpha_{v}}\right)\Omega[\Delta_{\mathsf{r}}]\right)I^{*}

(A.6)

\displaystyle I^{*}

\displaystyle=\pm\int_{\Delta_{\mathsf{r}^{*}}}\text{Res}_{B_{\mathrm{out}}=0}\left[\left(\prod_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{r}^{*}}}x_{v}^{\alpha_{v}}\right)\mathrm{dlog}B_{\mathrm{out}}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}B_{\mathrm{in}}\wedge\tilde{\Omega}[\Delta_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\uparrow}}]\wedge\tilde{\Omega}[\Delta_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\downarrow}}]\right]

\displaystyle B_{\mathrm{out}}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/Bout.pdf}=\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\uparrow}}x_{v}+\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\downarrow}}x_{v}+S_{\mathrm{out}}

\displaystyle B_{\mathrm{in}}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/Bup.pdf}=\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\uparrow}}x_{v}+f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\uparrow}}\qquad\mathrm{or}\qquad B_{\mathrm{in}}=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/Bdown.pdf}=\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\downarrow}}x_{v}+f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\downarrow}}\,,

where
\mathcal{V}_{\uparrow}=\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\uparrow}}
and
\mathcal{V}_{\downarrow}=\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\downarrow}}
.
To evaluate
I^{*}
, choose a
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\uparrow}
and a
v^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\downarrow}
.
Then, set

\displaystyle x_{v^{\prime}}

\displaystyle={-}\!\!\!\!\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\uparrow}\setminus v^{\prime}}\!\!x_{v}{-}f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\uparrow}}{+}z\,,\quad x_{v^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle={-}\!\!\!\!\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\downarrow}\setminus v^{\prime\prime}}\!\!x_{v}{-}f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}}{+}f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\uparrow}}{+}y{-}z\quad\text{if}\quad{\mathfrak{g}}=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/grg_simp.pdf}

(A.7)

\displaystyle x_{v^{\prime}}

\displaystyle={-}\!\!\!\!\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\downarrow}\setminus v^{\prime}}\!\!x_{v}{-}f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\downarrow}}{+}z\,,\quad x_{v^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle={-}\!\!\!\!\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\uparrow}\setminus v^{\prime\prime}}\!\!x_{v}{-}f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}}{+}f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\downarrow}}{+}y{-}z\quad\text{if}\quad{\mathfrak{g}}=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/glg_simp.pdf}

After this change of variables,
B_{\mathrm{out}}=y
,
B_{\mathrm{in}}=z
and

\displaystyle I^{*}

\displaystyle\propto\int_{\Delta_{\mathsf{r}^{*}}}\text{Res}_{y=0}\left[\left(\prod_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{r}^{*}}}x_{v}^{\alpha_{v}}\right)\mathrm{dlog}\,y\wedge\mathrm{dlog}\,z\wedge\tilde{\Omega}[\Delta_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\uparrow}}]\wedge\tilde{\Omega}[\Delta_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\downarrow}}]\right]

(A.8)

Specializing to the case where
B_{\mathrm{in}}=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/Bup.pdf}
,

\displaystyle I^{*}

\displaystyle\propto\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{dlog}\,z\;I^{*}_{\uparrow}I^{*}_{\downarrow}

(A.9)

where

\displaystyle I^{*}_{\uparrow}

\displaystyle\propto\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}^{|\mathcal{V}_{\uparrow}|-1}x_{\uparrow}\;\;\theta\left(\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\uparrow}\setminus v^{\prime}}\!\!x_{v}{+}f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\uparrow}}{-}z\right)\left({-}\!\!\!\!\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\uparrow}\setminus v^{\prime}}\!\!x_{v}{-}f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\uparrow}}{+}z\right)^{\alpha_{v^{\prime}}-1}\left(\prod_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\uparrow}\setminus v^{\prime}}\theta(-x_{v})x_{v}^{\alpha_{v}-1}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\propto\theta\big(f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\uparrow}}{-}z\big)\big({-}f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\uparrow}}{+}z\big)^{\alpha_{\uparrow}}\frac{\prod_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\uparrow}}\Gamma(\alpha_{v})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{\uparrow})}\,,

(A.10)

and

\displaystyle I^{*}_{\downarrow}

\displaystyle\propto\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}^{|\mathcal{V}_{\downarrow}|-1}x_{\downarrow}\;\;\theta\left(\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\downarrow}\setminus v^{\prime\prime}}\!\!x_{v}{+}f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}}{-}f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\uparrow}}{+}z\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt\times\left({-}\!\!\!\!\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\downarrow}\setminus v^{\prime\prime}}\!\!x_{v}{-}f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}}{+}f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\uparrow}}{-}z\right)^{\alpha_{v^{\prime\prime}}-1}\left(\prod_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\downarrow}\setminus v^{\prime\prime}}\!\!\!\!\theta(-x_{v})x_{v}^{\alpha_{v}-1}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\propto\theta\big(f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}}{-}f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\uparrow}}{+}z\big)\big({-}f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}}{+}f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\uparrow}}{-}z\big)^{\alpha_{\downarrow}}\frac{\prod_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\downarrow}}\Gamma(\alpha_{v})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{\downarrow})}\,,

(A.11)

where
\alpha_{\uparrow(\downarrow)}=\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\uparrow(\downarrow)}}\alpha_{v}
.
Putting things together,

\displaystyle W_{1}^{\mathrm{MPL,\alpha}}\Big[{[}\gamma_{{\mathfrak{h}}}|\varphi_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\rangle\Big]

\displaystyle\propto|{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}|\left(\prod_{\mathsf{r}\in\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{h}}\setminus\mathsf{r}^{*}}\left(f_{\mathsf{r}}^{\alpha_{\mathsf{r}}}\frac{\prod_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{r}}}\Gamma(\alpha_{v})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{\mathsf{r}})}\right)\right)\frac{\prod_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\uparrow}}\Gamma(\alpha_{v})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{\uparrow})}\frac{\prod_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\downarrow}}\Gamma(\alpha_{v})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{\downarrow})}

(A.12)

\displaystyle\times\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{dlog}\,z\;\theta\big(f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\uparrow}}{-}z\big)\big({-}f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\uparrow}}{+}z\big)^{\alpha_{\uparrow}}\theta\big(f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}}{-}f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\uparrow}}{+}z\big)\big({-}f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}}{+}f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\uparrow}}{-}z\big)^{\alpha_{\downarrow}}

Keeping only the leading term in the
\alpha_{\bullet}
, yields

\displaystyle W_{1}^{\mathrm{MPL,\alpha}}\Big[{[}\gamma_{{\mathfrak{h}}}|\varphi_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\rangle\Big]

\displaystyle\propto C_{{\mathfrak{g}}{\mathfrak{g}}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{dlog}\,z\;\theta\big(f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\uparrow}}{-}z\big)\theta\big(f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}}{-}f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\uparrow}}{+}z\big)\propto C_{{\mathfrak{g}}{\mathfrak{g}}}\log\frac{f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\uparrow}}}{f_{\mathsf{r}^{*}_{\downarrow}}}

(A.13)

where

\displaystyle|{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathfrak{g}}|\prod_{\mathsf{r}\in\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\left(\frac{\prod_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{r}}}\Gamma(\alpha_{v})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{\mathsf{r}})}\right)=C_{{\mathfrak{g}}{\mathfrak{g}}}\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\alpha_{\bullet}\right)\right)\,.

(A.14)

Performing the same computation for
B_{\mathrm{in}}=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/Bdown.pdf}
and reinserting the overall sign by carefully keeping track of the wedge product manipulations yields

\displaystyle W_{1}^{\mathrm{MPL,\alpha}}\Big[[\gamma_{\mathfrak{h}}|\varphi_{\mathfrak{g}}\rangle\Big]

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}C_{{\mathfrak{g}}{\mathfrak{g}}}\log\frac{f_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/f_u_right_dc.pdf}}}{f_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/f_uc_right_d.pdf}}}&\mathfrak{h}=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/gsg.pdf}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad{\mathfrak{g}}=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/grg.pdf}\,,\\[10.00002pt]
C_{{\mathfrak{g}}{\mathfrak{g}}}\log\frac{f_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/f_uc_left_d.pdf}}}{f_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/f_u_left_dc.pdf}}}&\mathfrak{h}=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/gsg.pdf}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad{\mathfrak{g}}=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figs/glg.pdf}\,.\end{cases}

(A.15)
